LADIES' LETTEM

MY DEAR NELLIE,

With colder weather we must adopt warless headgear than the lacy flower-wreathed millinery that has been adorning the world of fashionable women. The fur hat, or toque, has come, and its varied forms are so engaging that she would be more than human who could turn from its charm and bid the cosy wintery confection to wait a more fitting season. On many hats huge chrysanthemums take the place of the erstwhile gigantic rose, and though warm tonings will be seen, it is the lightly-tinted blossoms that will first have an innings. Naturally, many chrysanthemums will be worn, for this is the chrysanthemum season, but the sprinkling of other years is likely to become a deluge in this. The curling petals, however long, giving a less ragged effect is the species for millinery. The large velvet hat of winter, without which some would feel destitute, is decidedly of the Gainsborough type. Both in crown and brim it is modified, and the edge of the brim is bound with a wide silk braid of coarse plaiting. The very long feather of simple richness, or that backed with others so as to in- crease its fulness, sweeps along the left side of the upturned brim, the stem fixing beneath the braid border with a tiny buckle of glistening diamonds, and the end dropped gracefully on to the hair. The white feather on the hat of black, the natural shade to adorn fawn and brown, the lighter or the darker tone of the rival blues, will claim their respective admirers, and aid in the pretty millinery scheme for thr days just at hand.

I notice that all the new woollen materials are very soft and silky, and excellent for drap;ng, but distinguished by the long hairs on the surface. Some are shot, or changeant, shading from pale heliotrope to a rich velvet, or blue will be shot with green, green with purple, to give a few examples. Small undecided checks, pin stripes, and irregular-sized spots are all having a vogue in fashionable quarters. Brown and beaver shades are the leading tones for early autumn wear, and are much trimmed with black or very dark brown velvet, but cheerful fraise tints, old rose, and blue will be seen later on. There seems to be a strong feeling for rich, strong colours, which is just as it should be for dreary winter weather.

Cloth dresses are being trimmed with garlands and appliqua of beautifully-tinted flowers. A particularly good example was a gown of light brown cloth, the skirt trimmed with a couple of garlands of velvet flowers and foliage, in shades of pink mauve, and the tender green of the leaves, in the shape of a panier, pointing downwards in front and rounding off behind towards the top of the skirt, The bodice was encircled with bands of the velvet flowers, simulating a short rounded bolero, with a small rounded collar of cream cloth, bor- dered with a scalloped edging of russet-brown velvet, opening over a smocked chemisette of mauve mousseline de soie, whilst a narrow stitched band of the cream clpth and brown velvet finished •off the waist.

No shirt is safer for all changeable climates than a flannel one. This autumn a shirt is fitted with exquisite precision about the shoulders, which is given strap-like braces of silk passed from the centre of the back over the shoulders, there to meet two little pockets. Coat sleeves strapped with silk at the wrists are part of the scheme. Vague colours, such as sand, mouse-brown, silver- grey, and the deeper, duller purples, are good selections.

It is being tumbled about on chairs, or being heaped up one above the other on hooks, on doors, and in cupboards that ruins clothes most, and not the actual wear to which they are subjected. clothing should be exposed every now and then to the air when not in use. Nothing preserves its freshness better, or is more effectual as a pre- ventive of the ravages of moths. Gloves should never be rolled up together in a little bundle, but should be neatly smoothed out and laid away in a case or box. Veils should be folded in long narrow rolls each time they have been used. This will keep them in good condition for a long time. .

Your loving JENsis

OUR FIRST AUTUMN SALE

A GIRL OF .THE PEOPLE.

- - .•••••••r. By MRS. C. N. WILLIAMSON, Author of "The Barn Stormers," "Fortune's Sport," "Miss Nobody," "Her Royal High- Psis' " "Lady Mary of the Dark House," &e.

CHAPTER XXI. • TIIE row= BEIIIED TIIE THRONE. , I caught my breath. In an instant I had for- gotten the portraits, and had only eyes for what should come out from behind the curtains.

Then they were swept aside; and I saw the man whom I might perhaps have expected, yet had not dreamt of seeing—Roger Cope. Our eyes met. instantly all impression of mystery was gone. I felt that I should have knowis from the first that it would be explained in this way. "My darling—at last!" he exclaimed, and came towards me quickly, with both hands held out. But I put mine behind my back. "So you are the 'employer' of whom that woman spoke !" I said, viciously; "and this is your house." "I thought you might have guessed by this time whose place it' was," Roger answered. "Many things might have told you, even if you had for- gotten seeing the old photographs of the house taken from the outside, and the great hall with the pillars, which Aunt Ermyntrude kept. It was my Coiisin Vincent's place, you know, Mtn on his death it came to me with the title."

Now I understood why I had felt last night that I had lived through the scene of my arrival before; why I had known where to look for the staircase, and why, when I had passed out of the hall, the impression of familiarity disappeared. 1 had seen the photographs of which Roger spoke; but so long ago that my forgetfulness was scarcely surprising. "And then, all your pretty gowns and yoursilver -things, and jewels," Roger went on ; "who but one who loved you would have thought of having them ready fer your arrival ? And who on all this earth loves you as I do ? I was half afraid, half anxious that you should guess before seeing me whom you were to meet to-day. Oh. Sheila, you don't know what pleasure I took in securing all your things for you—even in the jewellery which the wretched girl Fanny Newlyn confessed to having pawned, and the new clothes which your mother told me you hod taken away to sell. And the room where you slept last night had everything that was there taken out-and new things put in—things which I hoped you would like. You can never doubt my love for you after to-day." ' I was bewilddred still by the far-reaching clever- ness of the trick which had ended by bringing me here. It seemed impossible to me that rove, and only love, had prompted a man of Roger Cope's type to such desperate measures; and I understood neither the means by which he had succeeded in carrying out his purpose, why it had been neces- sary to carry it out at all, nor what he intended to do with me now that I was here. But I wished to understand, and I controlled the angry words that rushed to my lips, meaning to keep them back until I had found out how far Roger was ready to explain. It even entered my mind that Roger might be mad; and the thought added to the need for caution.

"Yours is very Strange love," I said, quietly, though my voice was unsteady and a slight trembling shook my body. "And you have taken a strange way of shewing it. I don't understand exactly what the way has been, even now." "You shall understand if you like," he returned, "though there are- other things of which I would rather speak, things Which I have been waiting impatiently far a long, long time to say." "I should like to understand," I repeated, ignor- ing his last words. "Sit down, then, by my side, on this sofa. Oh, don't look frightened. I don't mean to force you. But if you won't do what 1 ask you can't expect me to give up my will to yours." Without further objections I sat down and Roger sat beside me. I saw him glance at my hands as if he contemplatid possessing himself of one; but hurriedly I clasped them together in my lap, and Roger attempted no more aggression. "Now catechise me, if you choose," he said, "and I will do my best to answer. Afterwards— but there will be time to talk of the afterwards ' when it has become the present. Begin, my little cousin: I am in a mood to be frank, now that luck has turned my way at last." I hoped that he might prove mistaken in believ- ing himself so favoured, as far as his boasted luck had to do with me. But I did not stop to contra- dict him, for I was anxious to come at the truth which lay at the bottoinlof so'dark, so deep a well. "Did you know where I went after I left Easel- street?" I asked.

"Not at first. I only wish that I had known. I called at Easel-street on the 'evening of that most eventful day because I couldWt help it, because I couldn't keep away from you. And I had to hear that hardly an hour before you had gone. You can guess a little of what. I felt. And I assure you that Mistress Fanny was obliged to listen tO some home truths for her brutality." His words convinced me that John Bourke had been right in his conjectures. Roger had set fire to the train ' • but the result, just as Mr. Bourke had thought, had come sooner, than it was expected. Roger had called, but had been too late to prevent me from leaving the home where I was not wanted. "I believe that Tom Stephens, Fan's 'pretended lover, was a tool of yours," I exclaimed, echoing the suggestion that had come from John Bourke. Roger smiled. "Rather clever for a child like you to have thrashed that out in your mind," he said. "I don't mind saying that you are right, for employingeaueenow any means in his power—even means ever thought the less of a man that would be called unscrupulous—to win her for himself. Yes, Torn Stephens was a 'tool ' of mine, as you put it., "He spied on me, and prejudiced the managers of employment agencies against me so that I was unable to get work?" "Yes. For if you had found work the end would have been delayed: I was cruel only to be kind. Tom Stephens managed matters very well, and though they went rather further than,l expected that, was hardly his fault." "Was it he who discovered where I had gone after I left Easel-street? " "No, poor Tom was not skilled detective eneugh for that. I had to get another man. It took him three days to find you out. Mr. John Bourke, M.P., has my gratitude for saving you—for me. I should never have 'forgiven Myself for what I had done, if you had succeeded in destroying your precious life. 'But ray gratitude to him ends with that episode. His later acts towards you were those of a villain; and it Wa4 all I could do not to go myself to his house and snatch my innocent white dove from the'elaws of the hawk." . The blood sprang to my face and burnt there. "It is like you to speak of him so!" 1 exclaimed„ fiercely. "He was 'an angel to roe, while you—ohl I donrt yet half understand what you have been or may be. I only know that you are not worthy to be spoken of in the same breath with John Bourke." It was Roger's turn now to flush, which he did, slowly, and with tightening lips. "It would please me best if you need trot speak of him at all or think of him," he said, evidently ntrolling him- self with an iron hand. "Is there any more Cate- chising to be gone through, or are you ready to give me the chance which I have earned?" "There are many Questions yet," I hurriedly returned, "and ,I can think of nothing else till you have answered them. Tell me, since' it seems to have distressed you so much that I should be at— at Mrs. Jennett's, not Mr. 'Bourke's, house, why you did not come yoKself and shew me reasons for leaving there ? " - "Because I was sure that, feeling towards me as you But ed i are anything which I could say wouldbe in v ou Cousin' just now, as of old. B ger Amin& have no guardian- ship, ncalledoy ot no authority,, no tangible right to command your actions ; at least. I had none until you came to this house. I could not force you to break with John Bourke against your own will, and, therefore, for your goiid, oblie,ed to proceed in a more subtle way." A new thought was born in my brain. "Was Lady No Ringwood another tool of yours'?" I deivanded. "She was not aware of being such. Nevertheless, grateful to me, slheuisia,ed fhorer. YOU yoonigt b ih lye ay 1 chose was s clever one, I think, and cost me a day or two of thought before I hit upon it. Yet, once seen, it was obvious, for everyone who knows Lady Poo well, knows that she is in love with Bourke and he with her—or, at all events that he NVil I marry her." I started slightly at ;he words, :tnd blushed because I knew that Roger had seen the start. "I had never heard of your acquaintance with Lady lido," I said, quickly, to hide what I felt. "I have known her, more or less, for years ; and thour.ii I have never nlet Bourke, we have mutual

acquaintances. I had but to call on Lady F6o,' hint that enemies of Bourke's had got hold of a -agdal_co,NnectWArilPWlTP-- iyeterious girl who 'Nelittio*On 4,the machinery ' Id go to him; well if some it a moment's tlessly ruined „ was to suppose me i,inoitint of the ,:tY, and no doubt she thought the denouement all the more dramatic because the information on which she acted had come from me. When I was sure that she would go to Bourke's house, a telegram was sent, .calling him out, leaving you at Lady Feo's mercy: I thought the rest might be trusted to her. And my only ether act of interference was to request- that, she should take with her a certain newspaper, to be left at the house when elib went away. I alleged, as an excuse for this suggestion, a paragraph which I had inserted; through the influence of a friend, mentioning Bourke's name and aimmour regarding him. That was for Lady.Feo Ringwood s eye. The advertisement in the 'personal column was for yoUrs, and I hoped that, if you had not seen it before, you would see it then. As it turned out, my wish was granted." ,• "Yes, I walked into the trap," I answered, bitterly. "I knew well enough that there might be a trap when I went to the Marble Arch, but I (Nd not guess that your hand had set it. There is dust one thing, though, which I can now be thankful for. Your confession' has shewn me that I haven't really injured Mr. Bourke. The enemies of whom Lady Feo spoke, and the scandal, existed only in your mind. I thank Heaven for that." "Then you are premature in your gratitude," Roger retorted, the angelic cast of his features betraying the emotion of anger for the first time. "Scandal was busy enough; and Bourke has many more powerful enemies than friends, if only he knew it. As a Matter of fact, I have saved him as well as you—though he owes me no thanks for that, since all I did was done for you, with no thought of his salvation." "It seems to me that all you did was for yourseli, with no thought of anyone else's welfare," I cried. "You have been very wicked, very cruel, Roger— even more cruel and wicked than 1 dreamt."

; "That is not a gracious speech, considering that my 'confession,' as you call it, has been made to please you," Roger responded. "It is incorrect to speak of it all as a 'confession.' I have told you the truth, answering with complete frankness every question you have asked, because I chose to do so, not because I was compelled to it by any sense of shame. On the contrary, I glory in what I have done—glory in each step that has brought me to success at last."

"It has not brought you to success, if you mean by that brought us nearer together," I protested. "Though I am in your house, and you could touch me with your hand—if yon wished to offend me even more deeply—l was never further from you really than at this moment." Roger gave a smile meant to be patient and pathetic. "That would sound very well," he said, "if you were the persecuted heroine in a melo- drama and I the villain of the piece. But as you are actually a human girl, who has made a great many mistakes, and been at last brought into a safe haven against your obstinate little will by a man who worships you and has worked only for your good, the sentiment is florid rather than theatrical. You were all alone in the world, darling, and your poor little boat was on the rocks; I waded out into the deep water, and risked a great deal to bring it safely off again. Now, here you are in your own house, which is mine as well, and here you will stay until your name is the same as one which once was "dear to you." "What do you mean?" I stammered, between fear and anger. "I mean, until I have the right to call you wife, the world to speak of you as Lady Cope." I sprang up from the sofa where I had resigned myself to sit questioning the enemy. "That will be never!" I cried. "Roger, if you want me ever to forgive you, you won't speak of that again ; and you will tell me the things you have so far carefully left untold." I pointed to the portraits that framed the curtained doorway— above and on either side. "You are in the mystery of the heart-shaped scar!" I went on. "I believe now that you have always known. Perhaps, though that woman, Sintra Leigh, says you did not employ her then, you really sent her to the theatre on the night when my adopted mother died. You must tell me the truth." Roger had risen when I rose, and we stood facing each other. "Do you think," he asked, with aggravating slowness, "that you have taken the wisest way of dealing with me, Sheila? When a woman of tact desires something from a man, she doesn't demand it with blazing eyes as her right; she softly begs it as a favour, shewing that she may be ready with rewards.'" _ .

"You certainly deserve a reward," I retorted, "but not such a one as you mean. And the truth is my right. jl do demand it. I chine to this house because it was promised to me." "Not without conditions. Listen, Sheila. There is a mystery. So much lam ready to admit; and I have known of.its existence for many years. If I told you all that is in my mind, you would under- stand very much that must otherwise remain dark and puzzling to you through your life. You would even understand why Aunt Ermyntrude left you cnr- the night she died, and why she died—though with all that I swear to you I had nothing to do. lam ready to tell everything you wish to know—not to Sheila or 'Jenny Harland,' but to my wife, Sheila Cope. Is it a bargain?" "No, it is not," I said. "If you won't tell me, I must find out in some other way." "You will not find out in any other way." His voice was suddenly tierce, and my eyes were forced to meet his and dwell upon his widening pupils. We looked at each other in silence. Then I spoke out my thoughts, with sudden passionate impulse. "Oh, what a fool I was to come here !" I cried. "If I had only kept my wits about me, and remembered that my father's—l mean that Sir Vincent Cope's place, which I had never seen, was in this direction, I might have suspected that you were concerned in this. But I supposed the place was still let to strangers, as it has been, I know, for years—" "I had to have a nest ready for the bird when it should flutter home," Roger broke in, smiling. "I wonder if any man since the world began ever conquered more difficulties for the woman he loved ? "

"I should think none ever stooped to so many meannesses," I flung at him. Other sharp words were ready, like swords, to pierce the armour of his self-complacence, but suddenly a voice seemed to whisper in my ear. "A strange nest for a bird," it said. "Roger had another use for the house. flow about those sounds outside your door last night ? "

did not repeat to Roger what the imaginary Voice had whispered, for I knew that not only would he be careful never to give me a true eitklanation of what I had heard, but would probably take steps to prevent my finding out for myself, as it now occurred to me that 1 might do. Roger's own .words about the "nest he had prepared for the bird "had put the thought into my head ; for the whisper I had fancied as an answer to his speech suggested another more subtle reason for his occupation of the house formerly let, to strangers. Somebody was hidden here, perhaps; the somebody who had groaned, and cried the name of Ermyntrude ; somebody who might tell me more than Roger chose to tell without a bribe. • I said•no more about trying to learn the secret in "another way," though such an idea was taking form in my mind; and so deeply was I absorbed in may own thoughts that Roger talked on for a few moments unheard. I was only conscious that he spoke, until suddenly his raised voice, evidently repeating a question that bad been asked before, brought .ine to myself. _

Do you understand what it Means Ithen I tell you that by to-morrow I expect to have the special licence for which I have applied? Roger had offered to let me speak with "Miss Leigh," and, though he did not appear well pleased at' having been finally taken at his word, he did not attempt to repudiate it. "Miss Leigh shall come to you here,' he said. Crossing the room, lie pulled an old-fashioned bell-rope. A far away jangling followed, and presently the grim-faced woman Who had brought my breakfast knocked at the door. She was abruptly commanded to "send Miss Leigh," and it was not long before my travelling companion of last night appeared. "I will leave you together, " said Roger. "But before going, perhaps fhad better make it clear that Sheila wishes you to explain why it has now become absolutely imperative that she should marry me." As he spoke he was at the door, and an instant later it had closed behind him.

"I should have thought that, to a young woman of your intelligence, such explanations were unnecessary," said the woman in black. "But I can give them in a few words. Already, it seems, you have afforded your friends and enemies great cause for gossip. Sir Roger Cope has offered you the chance of rehabilitating yourself. He was obliged to use rather strong measures to accomplish his purpose, and if you do not fall in with his way of thinking, instead of matters being improved, they will have been made far worse. You are at his house ; everyone knows that it is his house ; the servants and others know that you have been here since last evening—that you came willingly. What will become ,of you if, after all this, you refuse to marry him ?" "So this is what he preferred to have you explain!" I exclaimed. "I don't wonder that even Roger Cope hesitated to say it himself." "it may not be pleasant to hear, but it is the truth. I gave you very good advice when I brought you to the door of this room an hour ago. lam sorry that you don't seem to have remembered it very well, but it is not too late yet. Aslsaid then, you have everything to lose, or everything to gain. Money, two charming houses,, the ability to help your relatives (who from accounts I have heard need all the help that can be given them) an enviable position in society, with past indiscretions forgotten. All this you have, on the one band; on the other—but perhaps that picture is best left to your imagination." For a moment I did oot speak. Then I said, shortly: "Please call Sir Roger back again."

He was summoned, and presently appeared, the woman in black meanwhile asking no questions, though her gipsy face expressed curiosity as to my intentions.

"Has Miss Leigh's eloquence been more fortunate than mine, in persuading you that there's only one course open " Roger asked. "She has shewn tne that all others are difficult," I answered. "Still, if I were to decide at once, I should say : 'No, I cannot marry you.' That's my impulse now, but if you will give me your word of honour to go away from here—back to London or to Arrish Moll Court, whichever you choose— for two days, I will stop and think the matter carefully over. Miss Leigh can give me as much gooa advice as you wish, and I may change my mind."

Ronr shrugged his shoulders. "The longer you stay here the less easy will it be for you to decide against me," he said; "so I suppose I shall be wise to give you your own way. But we wili com- promise matters. I'll go and come back to-morrow —with the special licence of which I told you." " Very well, then, to-morrow," I assented. "But you must gcl now. "If you look out of this window—or one in your own room—in fifteen minUtes from now, you will see me driving away from the house." "With one of the new carriages, and the new livery you have bought with Lady Cope's money," it was on the tip of my tongue to say. But I kept the words back. For once, Roger vita true to his word. Whether he meant to leave the neighbourhood or not, I had no means of knowing; but at least he drove away from the house, with a small portmanteau obstenta- tiously placed Where I would be mire to see it. . . . .

- . I knee/ that he had only not attempted to put nie upon parole as a prisoner because he was sure that I should not be able to evade the guard he had dortbtless set upon my movements. But I was glad that he had not tried to exact a promise, because, as soon as I had made a desperate effort to discover the secret which I had begun to believe might be hidden in this house, I intended to make an equally desperate bid for freedom. Perhaps if I had not been taken for my interview with Roger into the room with the three portraits, no inkling of the idea which now so keenly excited me might ever have come into my head. But there had been Sir Vincent Cope, with Sintra Leigh, and the man who so marvellously resembled her. All three pictures had apparently been painted at aboat the same time, for certain mannerisms suggested that they were the work of the same artist, and the frames with which they were set into the wall were identical in detail. This suggested' that Sintra -Leigh and her double (a twin brother, my fancy already said) were closely connected with the Cope family either by ties of bloqd or peculiarly intimate friendship. Sintra Leigh had on her arm the heart-shaped scar, which seemed to me to be the secret incarnate. Here she was, in the house where her,portrait as a young girl had been preserved for so many years. But where was the original of the third portrait Could it be that he was also under this roof ? Was it his voice that had cried the name of Ermyntrude in the night, before he had again been hurried away into obscurity? If it were true that such a man was voluntarily or involuntarily a guest in the house which had once been Sir Vincent, Cope's, apparently his presence was to be kept a secret from me. Sintra Leigh had, I was certain, lied when I questioned her concerning the strange sounds in the night. Therefore something was to be hidden from me, and it was natural to suppose that the something 'had to do with the secret. Roger knew the secret, and did not wish me to know it; and for this reason, as much as any other, it seemed worth While to make some sacrifices and run some risks, for the sake of even one chance out of a hundred that 1 might stumble upon it. Sintra Leigh and I had a large, pleasant dining_ room to ourselves, save for the old butler, who had admitted us to the house last night. Once 1 ventured to ask whether she had known this place for long, but she evaded the quastion, and .I did not think it prudent to raise suspicion by pressing her. When we had finished and left the dining-room, I heard the door being softly locked after us on the inside, Instantly I guessed that the butler had been instructed to do this; for the windows opened on to the lawn, and it would be easy enough for anyone who wished to walk out of the house. The stealthy sound reminded me of the teat I had intended to apply, and I asked Sintra Leigh if she would take me through some of the principal rooms. "if I decide to marry Sir Roger Cope,' I remarked, rather flippantly, "this will be my house, ,as you impressed upon me last night; and naturally, I should like to know something of the •sargain I may be making.' ,-You shall see the whole house if you wish,* said the wonsan in black. We were in the great hall with the ugly pillars when I made the request, and Sintra Leigh rang .:-. bell which presently brought the butler. "The keys of the rooms on this floor," she said ; where- upon a bunch of large, old-fashioned keys wai produced, from a pocket and respectfully handed -to her.

-- -- "This is the way, then, of unatrusively making me a prisoner," I thought; but I made no comment aloud, and only requested, with due meekness, that: I might see the rooms on higher floors as well. Miss Leigh announced that the other keys which might be peeded were in her own possession ; and forthwith we began an inspection of the rooms or, the ground-floor. "The house is so large and thert. ism so few persons in it at present," my companion deigned to explain, "that Sir Roger considers it more prudent, to keep the unused rooms locked, except when they are wanted." Still I made no comment, but I thought that I understood. We visited a number of handsome rooms, all furnished in the style of long ago, and I simulated more interest than I really felt. The place had, it is time, a certain charm of association for me because it had been Sir Vincent Cope's; and as my 3dopted mother had been a cousin of the man she married, I doubted not that she had been in this house, though Arrish Plat! ' At last we went upstairs, each door having been. locked as we left the room to which it belonged; and here my interest quickened, as in a childish game of hide and seek, when the players cry "warm" or "cold" to eaoh other.

If there were in the house a man whose presence, was to be concealed, it was probable that his rooms were on one of the upper floors, and each closed door that we reached might be hiding a mystery. On the first-floor some doors were looked, others stood open, as I had noticed last night on my arrival. But I soon satisfied myself that the rooms 'which were not secured against intrusion offered no facilities for escape, even to a courageous and determined person. Their windows without exception looked out upon a courtyard, from which evidently there was no means of egress, save by returning through the house. As for my 'own room, anyone attempting to escape by the windows would have to- descend into a moat, which was half full of water, carpeted with the flat green leaves and waxen bucie of lilies.

My zeal for exploration apparently knew no bounds, for I did not pass a single door without begging to see what was on the other side ; and, with a weary air of resignation, the woman in black each time granted my request. After I had seen everything on the first-floor, I asked to visit the second. Sintra Leigh offered no objection, save & sigh; and again the exploration was exhaustive. "Now there are only the third-floor and the two towers left," I remarked at last.

"I hardly think that the third-floor will interest you," said my companion. "The servants sleep there. And as for the towers, they have been used for a long time as places to store all sort of rubbish which 'will accumulate in old houses as generation after generation passes." "Nevertheless, if there's a chance of my being mistress here, everything should be of interest to me," I returned; and with another sigh Miss Leigh preceded tne upstairs. She threw open door after door, and, as she had prophesied, the tour of inspection became more than ever dull. I glanced into the servants' rooms, not because I wished in the least to see them, but because I wanted to be sure that they really were what they purported to be. The part of the house occupied by the servants was in a separate wing, but the remaining portion of the third-floor was not much superior in its attractions, at all events to my preoccupied mind. At last, however, the woman in black attempted rather hastily to pass a door without opening it. "What room is that ? " I promptly demanded. "I believe that Sir Roger Cope gave the late tenants the privilege of leaving a few things in the house for a time, until it should be more con- venient to remove them," she explained, with an air of complete indifference. "They are kept in that room, and Sir Roger has the key. Now, if you like, we will go on to the towers. There is rather- a good view from the windows, but I warn you that there is nothing else you will care for." . _ _

As she spoke she -walkea away from the door of which Sir Roger was supposed to have the key, and I did not attempt to detain her. I thought that at last I had found out what I wanted to know; for I did not believe that the room behind the locked door was sacred to the belongings of departed, tenants. I said to myself that there, if anywhere, lurked the Secret.

We visited the towers, which gave a wide view of the county of Dorsetshire towards Bournemouth, with a far-distant glimpse of the sea; and then Sintra Leigh reminded me that, unless I wished ty visit the kitchen and servants' hall, I hnd done all that there was to do. I did not wish to see the domain she mentioned, and consented to go down with her to the drawing-room for ten. I was glad that the hours were passing, for I knew that I must wait for night before attempting that which it was in my mind to do. I asked if it would, be convenient to have dinner at seven, as I was very tired, and would be glad to go to bed early, by way of making up for lack of sleep the night before. This was easy/ to arrange, Sintra Leigh responded; and in consequence of mil request we dined at the hour I had named. After— wards we had coffee in the room where Roger and I had talked in the morning, as (the woman ill black explained) it was more cheerful in the, evening than the huge drawing-room. During the scene with Roger my nerves had been keyed high with excitement; my eyes had fallen on the three portraits by the curtained door through which he had entered, and after that I had observed few details. But now that Sintra. Leigh and I sat together at leisure, I saw some— thing which I had not seen before—something that roused my interest and curiosity to the highest-. pi cola.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

With America, and things American, our thoughts have been much of late, and our sincerest sympathies have gone: out to that lonely, pathetic figure, who, bereft of children and husband, now stands desolate , indeed. All the world is aware, the late President was devoted to his fragi'e wife, and the to him, and those who know Mrs McKinley well, have very little hope that she will long survive her husbano. With all interest in life gone, her dectors have their most power- ful medicine wrested from them, and it seenis only too probable that the wretched asset sin wde very shortly, have yet another life to answer tor, v.z , that of his victim's wife. For many years past, needlework has been one el Mrs McKinley's greatest pleasures and mans beautiful and intricate forms has this art taken in her aands. As a worker for bazaars she has had a wide reputation and requests for " some email contribution " have been wont to reach her almost daily, from one part or ether, of the ccuntrv. A pair of worked slippers was the cus- tomary gift, and Mrs McKinley is said to have stated some two or three years ago, that she had then worked and bestowed upon some charity, more than 2,000 pairs. The King has had the full-s'zs, brorzs statue of the late John Brown removed from the grounds at Balmoral, where it steed in a prominent position. It has now been taken to Ealnachoie, the residence of Mr Willie Brown, the brother of the Royal servant who was be- lieved to have exercised so much influence at Court. The servants of the Household have now been placed by Lord Farquhar under a new regime, which is more strict than in the time of Queen Victoria. New munitions have been made with regard to leave, and hours of duty,and there has been a general reduction in travelling and board allowances. Formerly the upper servants were allowed first class, and all others second class expenses, but under the altered arrangments, third class will be the general rule. The discussion on " The Decay of Domesticity " in one of the London pay era has produced a variety of views upon the subject, but there is no doubt that the tendency in the great cities is all towards an altered condition of home life. The " servant question " has bad much to do ia creating the demand tor flata, and these are now being built in huge blocks, with public dining rooms attached, the establishment providing its own catering. Many people without families, prefer a two or three room flat in such establishments, which seem to offer the least troublesome method of living,and among all classes there seems an ever increasing desire to escape from the drudgery of the home. The Marquis of Anglesey, who is only twenty-se years of age, can scarcely be surprised to find himself the victim of a jewel robbery. Most women have a weakness for jewellery, but the Marquis is well known to have a passicn for it, and when ne leaves thirty or fifty thcusand pounds worth of valuables about hie rooms, in a private hotel, it is net surprising that be should sooner or later find some of it missing. The great wealth of his Staffordshire mineral property enables him to freely gratify his fondness for precious stones, and when as Lord Uxbridge, he married in 1893, his cousin Miss Chetwynd, he bestowed upon her an almost un- rivalled collecticn of emeralds and diamonds. Emeralds appear to be the Marquis of Anglesey's favourite gem, and Lady Anglesey counts among her rresouree in entirepareee of priceless specimeas. She created quite a sensation when she first wore them at a State ball she attended shortly n free her marriage, her gown for that occasion—of white gauzs with embroideries ot green tern and foliage—being " built up to the gems. Lady Anglesey is a very beautiful woman, and a general favourite in Society. Her hair which is wavy and abundant is of Titian red, and th,i Marchioness is careful always to give it a becomiog background, by donning suitable contrasts in costume If one may judge from the prices quoted for the skins and feathers of wild birds, not much has been done, I either by Act of Parliament, or the cu cry against " murderous millinery,' to protect the birds from indis- criminate slaughter. Four a penny is the price quoted for crested starlings, while swallows, kingfishers, and woodpeckers, are to be had for eight a penny. One is surprised to find that the kingfisher has not a higher value, seeing that all authorities are agreed that this beautiful bird is becoming very rare in this country. Egret plumes it seems, do not fetch quite so much as formerlY, and sometimes shop keepers reverse the usual order of things, and sell the real article as an artificial one, in order to meet the objection of their more humane customers. Possibly, if we were all to talk a little less, and act a little more, matters in this direc- tion might improve. There are many other forms of " trimming " available for headgear. about which no possible question of cruelty can arise. When there is no demand for birds, wings, and egret plumes, there will anon be no supply. Really we hold the matter in our own hands. Between the dressmakers and the ladies tailors there is a constant struggle to di minate the fashions of the period. This sear the dressmakers have had things greatly their own way, but winter stylee once more 1 favour tailor-made garments' For these, warm, soft cheviots, friez-s, and cloths will be largely used, whilst colors seem likely to run upon the darker reds,and blues. Black and semi-mourning having been the prevailing modes of the year, the tendency is now towards re- Refiner, and a somewhat extravagant display of colours. The loose, semi-fltting coat of three quarter length, still finds the greatest favour, and boasts little ("save in the way of stitchings) in the matter of ornamentation. Certain bead chains, treasured by us. as being the work of our patient fore-mother., have been long laid by, and are now again in fashion. Spheres and ovoids and etranee undefinable shapes in gems and glass and bone, have yielded place to plaits like wide flat braid. A few large beads appear at regular intervals but the I centre band is of rows and rows of tiny heads. plaited I together for some inches and then caught in with one of tbe larger balls. There is infinite variety to be had in colours, and skilful workers with patterns before them, even elaborate floral and other designs, all in these weel coloured beads. In really old work a heavy bead fringe finished off the ends of the chains. They seem costly to buy, these ornaments, but rot to those who have prac- tised making thin, atd learnt what quantities of Material is re quire d. Loud laughter is said to speak the vaaant mind, but everyone knows that the faculty of laughter is an im- portant and desirable possession. If we have rot the necessary light-heartedness for whole some'andintelligent hilarity, the next thing, according to en American doctor who has made a speciality of nervous diseases,is to smile, and everyone can do that with more or less srceese. For some or other reason, American women are peculiarly prone to depression, and his main recom- mendation for nervous patients is simply to keep the corners of the mouth turned up instead of down, no matter what one'a feelings may be. Whether or not this is a cure for neyoum melancholy, it is advice that is anyhow worth treing to follow artificially.if it does not come naturally, sepirg that the secret of always looking young lies in a smiling cNuntenance. MILLIONS A YEAR.

Time Lost and Decreased Wages. The grievanca of immense losses sustained annually by skilled workers of every occupation is ly.‘coming more acute every year. Calculations show that the money out of workers' pockets. through hours and day! lcst, represents in wages many millions sterling per annunti, and the serious effect on the social comfort of the masses is evident. Mr Jenkins, factory worker sf Bristol, said the other day:—

" I have lose plenty of tune, enough, perhaps, to pay my rates; but I have learned better now, and I can give you the recipe if you like. For three or four years. until recently, I frequently lost time through a serious disorder of the stomach. I would be soizsd by dreadful an In the region of the abdomen, especially after a meal ; and at the same time racking pains in the head made me fear for my reason. PRESIDENT M‘KINLEY'S DEATH.

A Calm and Peaceful End.

Mr. M'liinley, President of the United States, died at Buff lo at a quarter-past two on Saturday morning, the English time being nearly half-past seven. The news came through very rapidly. De- tails of the closing scene show that the end came very quietly and peacefully. When Mr. APKinley felt that he was sinking he took farewell of Mrs. ArKinley and the others stand- ing around his bedside. He then looked round him, and his last words were, " Good-bye, all—all. Good-bye. It's God's way, and His will be done, not ours." Than evidently speaking to no one, tbe President said, " Nearer, my God, to Thee, e'en though it be a cross, is my cmstant prayer." Mr. /tPliinley then wandered in his p tiec b The following report of the autopsy has been issued by the physicians by whom it was made : Toe bullet which struck the President over the breast-bone did not pass through the skin, and did little harm. . . .

The other bullet, passed through both walls of the stomach near its lower border. Both holes were found to be perfectly closed by the 6titcheB,but the tissue around each hole had become gangrenous. After pa=sing through the stomach, the bullet passed into the back walls of abdomen, hitting and tearing the upper end of the kidney. This portion of the bullet track was also gangrenou.. the gan- grene involving the pancreas. The bullet has not yet been found. There was no sign of peritonitis or disease of other organs. There was no evidence of any attempt at repair on the part of Nature, and deatn resulted from the gangrene, which affected the stomach around the bullet wounds as well as the tissues around the further course of the bullet. Death was unavoidable by any surgical or medical treatment, and was the direct result of the bullet wound. .

Very many ms sages of sympathy have been sent to Mrs WKinley and to the American people from all parts of the world, King E iward, the German Emperor, and ether potentates having telegraphed their condolences. The English Court goes into mourning for a week. The New President.

Col. Roosevelt, the Vice-President, automaticaly suc- ceeds to the Presidency. He was hunting in the Adiron- dacks on Friday, but started for Buffalo on hearing of the critical condition of the President. He arrived some hours after Mr WKlnley's death, and in the afternoon he took the oath of his new ofil3e, in the presencs of the members of his Cabinet, whom be has asked to retain their offices for the present. Be announced that it would be his aim to continue absolutely unbroken the policy of Mr Rides, for the peace. prosperity, and honour of the United States. He is 43 years of age, and belongs to an aristocratic American family of Dutch origin. He is said ts be the first President of th 3 United States not of English or Scotch descent. The Assassin. True Bill against Czolgosz.

BUFFALO, SEPT. 16.

The grand jury has returned a true' bill against Cz^lgofz on the charge of murder in the first degree. Czolgo,z was to-day arraigned before the County Court. Be rqusod to plead. Mr Lorain L Lewis and Mr Robert C Titus, former Supreme Court Justices, have been appointed to defend the accused. When Cz-llgosz Wil3 being taken b ick to gaol the crowd surged about the police, who, however, succeeded in keeping them off, and in locking up the prisoner.

CONSERVATIVE CONDOLENCE.

At a meeting of the Conservative Cmnmittee of the Town Ball Ward. held in the committee room at the Conservative Club, on Monday evening, Councillor Smethurst. who presided, proposed a vote of condolence with Mrs McKinley and the American people in the loss they had sustained by the death of the President.—The motion was eccmded by Councillor A J Jolley, and carried.

===== 0002601_19010920_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEWS AT RUNCORN.

Early on Saturday 'notating word was flashed to Runcorn that President M'ilinley had breathed his last. Though the news was hardly unexpected, owing to the gloomy tone of the later bulletins, the announcement that the assassin's bullet had done its deadly work creat- ed a profound impression. It was not, however, until later in the day that the news became generally known, when an air of profound gloominess seemed to settle over the town. Flags floated at half mast and in several places the drawn blinds bore witness to the esteem with which the great American was regarded by his kinsmen. At the Runcorn Parish Church a mufti peal was rung and at the various places of worship on Sunday reference was made t the deceased statesman's memory. In connection with the Free Church Council a memorial service is to be held in which all the Non- conformist m.nisters will take part. Runcorn in com- pany with every town and bomlat in Eogland .ihares to the full the sorrow which has befallen America.

===== 0002601_19010920_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
Runcorn Petty Sessions.

At the Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monthy before the business of the clue commenced Mr A R Norman, who occupied the chair supported by Mr W TimrninN said that he was sure that he was expressing not only the feeling of every member of the bench but also all con- nected with the court, and indeed every respectable citizen when he laid they, felt indignation and sorrow when they received the news of the terrible tragedy which had taken place across the water. So far as they knew the only crime President McKinley had committed, wen in the eyes of the das— tardly assassin, was that he was faithfully and honour- ably discharging duties to which it had pleased Go' to call him. They sympathised and mourned with their kinsmen at this terrible event. They must all pray that some means could be speedily brought into opera- tion whereby such an incident as this migtt be made impossible in the future. That was their hope and wish.

Local References.

References were made at most of ths churches and chare's in Warrington on Sunday to the lamented death of President M•Binley. At St Paul's Church, the flag on:tbe_tower _flew at half-mast. . _

At the ,Parish Church in the evenine, the Rector alluded to the President's death from which he drew four lessons. What an immense responsibility attached to our words and writings ! These poor wretched crea- tures were not to blame so mu:sh as those who made in- flammatory speeches and wrote books which worked upon the passions of men. The second lesson was that the event would tend to oromote a still greater union between the two great nations—England and America. 'There bad been occasions when our relations had been strained, but of late they had been veonr friendly, and he had beard that the late President's influence had len directed towards that end. That fact would make , England still more sympathetic with America. We were one race, one language, one relleion to a great extent. The next lesson was that Mr McKinley's career showed how it was possible for all to rise. Apparently he be- longed to the working-class, and was of bumble origin, but he raised himself to the highest mition in the States by his own ability. That might be done in Eng- land. end there was no position short of the Throne to which a man might not rise by ability ; and, therefore. 'what God wanted them to do was to make good use of their opportunities. The last lesson was that hel was A religious man and was not ashamed of his God and Church. They were told that be always attended his place of worship, and bow very few of the working- classes seemed to act in a similar manner. What a sad thing it was to think that a man spent his whole life pleasing himself, and vet at the last begged God to for- give him and to take him to heaven ! Let them try to love and live with God here and then they might live with him hereafter. References were made at St. Paul's Church by the Revs ER Tarbuck and L W Thomas, arid by the Rev E C E Carleton at Holy Trinity. The Vicar (Rev F E Powell). Preached in St. James Church'en the test : "Sot God forbid that I should Only save in the Cross of our Lord Jesus Christ." He WO the Cross manifested the Divin love, as nothing else did or could; its contemplation gave pardon. peace. and joy to the sinful and distressed it touched hearts sod changed lives, and deprived death of its terror The President of the great Republic, victim to a brutal assassin, found all this in the Cross of Christ, while the murderer, once an innocent lad, could never have com- mitted so foul a crime, had be clung to the teachieg 'of the Crucified. Our deepest sympathies went out to the great nation which we were proud to know among from carrying with It our institutions, our sense of free- dom, our faith in the Cross of ebrist. Tbe Rev T Bishop (superintendent of the Warrington Circuit) preached at the Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Sunday eveaing from the words " Know ye not that there is a prince and a great man lane° this day in Israel." He said that they would expect him to say something that evening about that awful event—Presi- dent M'Kinley's death. The whole civilized world stood aghast, felt terrified,and vengeance was called for.

===== 0002601_19010920_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
IRE EXAMINER FRIDAY, SEPTEMBER 20 1901

In one short week they had a President assassinated, a weeping and invalid widow, a sorrowing nation, and a world in mourning. As a man ascended th 9 ladder of power, political, or social, he became mofe known, more watched and envied, and his lite almost ceaied to be private, his successes being minimiz -‘d and his failures exaggerated. He needed the ballast of prayer and must wait upon GA. The demands of business were so great nowadays that the danger was that reli- gion was shunted to let business go by. Prayer was either cut short or else forgotten. President M'Kinley was a Methodist, and a member of the Metropolitan Methodist Church, Washington. where also President Grant worshipped. He was regular in his attendance and loved a good sermon. When pleased he would say so, as he believed that encouragement never did harm to anyone. He was a good bearer, and was full of tenderness for little children. Many good stories were told which showed how dearly he luoed children. lie was elected by the suffrages of his people, not succeed- ing to his porition by hereditary right, and so had a greater claim on their love and sympathy. He had that love universally, for it was doubtful if another in that vast continent, with its enormous population of all peoples and languages could have been found to do such a miscreant's deed, and horrify the whole world by such aldastardly outrage. Precident M'Kinley was always fearless of danger ; he would never haven guard round him, because he so trusted others that it was impossible for him to believe than anyone would harm him. . .

Preaching at the Sankoy Bridges Wesleyan Church,nn Sunday morning, the Rev 0 II Hocken said i—Tne death of the President of the United States touches us as MethodiNts very closely, not only because Mr M•Kinley was the head of that great country united to us by the ties of blood, but also because he was a devoted rneinher of our own church and becaus 3 there are at present 300 distinguished American delegates of our Church now gathered in Conference in Wesley's Chapel, Llndon. This wanton outrage which has struck down, this noble man as Lincoln and Garfield were struck down, in a country which is the foremost exponent of freedom, fills us with horror and grief. We are smitten to the heart with our kinsmen. The Wesleyan Church is one of the chief connecting links of the Anglo-Saxon race, and nowhere are the bonds which connect us with the United States"stronger than among ourselves. We trust that this common sorrow will, under God, be the means of uniting our hearts still more closely, and we pray that the American peorle may he sustained and comforted in this great shock to their national life, and that Gel's special help and blessing may be given to the stricken widow, who is herself, in feeble health. We feel for them profound sympathy and unutterable sorrow. At the conclusion of the service the organist, Mr T Brong,h, played the Dead March in "Saul." Mr Hocken made a similar reference at the Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church In the evening, Mr Joseph Spruce, of Preston Brook, preached in the place lof the RP,i, C Finch at L igh Street Primitive Mathadist Church on Sunday morning. Before cominenchsg his sermon he alluded to the, terrible news which had been received from America on the preceding day. One of the great- est men of tie day had f alien, and by the hand of an assassin. Mr Spruce denounced anarchy and disorder, and said what a marvellous change would be effected if the principles of the Gospel were accepted and practised throughout the world. He felt sure that they all syinpathizsd with the American people in the disaster which bad befallen them and prayed that God would bless and comfort them.

The Rev Joint YONGE, in a brief reference at the morning service in Wycliffe Congregational Church where the annual harvest thanksgiving services were held on Sunday,said it might be a break in the harmnny of the service of thanksgiving and praise, but they could not pass without deeply sorrowful allusion to the terrible event that had plunged America into dismay and grief. When the news came that Pregident arl'llinley had been shot by an assassin a mighty thrill of horror passed through this and every other nation in the world. Relief was effected by the signs aff vied that the blow was not fatal. From day to day, the world had been hoping for the best, and fervent prayer had besought the life of the wounded President. But these hopes had been denied,and on the previous morn- ing this twice elected President breathed his last. It was singular and sad that he should be the third President of the United States who, in the lifetime of many of them had fallen by the hand of an assassin. Lincoln, Garfi.ld, M.Kinley—all good Christrian men, patriotic men, great men in character, in influence, in beneficence. Verily, tmerica had known her stupendous griefs, and Britain mourned by her side to-day. They prayed that the terribly stricken widow, herself in feeblest health, might be able to bear the impact of this blow, and that the be- reaved nation might be able to bear this grief avieli calmness and dignity, and be spared any outburst of violent vengeance against the assassin, or the men and women who had brought about this deed. "Vengeance. is mine ! I will repay, saith the Lord." The people of these realms did not forget America's sympathy in the national bereavement that befel us in January last. Our honoured Queen, who narrowly escaped like tragic fate mere tben OPCA in her long reign, died plac.fully In her bed. Our Sing himself not long since Immo,. ienced a narrow escape from assassination. The terrible experience, so far, had not befallen us, and we prayed it never would. But these things quickened and deepened our feeling for our kinsmen across the sea. and we mourned in their sorrow. God speed the day when such deeds shall become impossible by the uni- versal reign of the King of Love and the Prince of Peace. . . . . .

At the meeting of the Wycliffe P.S. A . Society, held under the chairmanship of Mr Thomas Wallington, a resolution of sympathy with Mrs MtKinley and the people of the United States was passed. The Rev John Young (president of the society) in the course of a brief reference to the event said he thought it would be a great mistake if a meeting of workingmen were suffered to pass by that day without special reference being made to the terrible and distressing event which had resulted in the death of the President of the United States. He felt sure that every Englishman, Scotsman, Welshman and Irishman who were seorthr of their country would sympathise with the Americans in tbe terrific blow which had crashed upon them. We did not forget how spontaneous and universal was the fe-ling displayed toward us by the American people at the beeinning of this year when we lost our beloved Sovereign Qieen Victoria. She was permitted to live a long life and sway the sceptre for many years. Narrowly herself escaping the hand of the assassin more than once, she came to the end of her long reign in peace. It had been given the American nation to sustain the loss of three Presidents cs ithin his (the speaker's) memory by the hand of the assassin. 16 was a terrible thing that in the land which was a home of freedom three Presidents elected by the people should be assassinated. He was sure they all protested against and abhorred such deeds, and he had therefore drawn up the following resolution for their approvol: "That this meeting of working men, mem- bers of the Wycliffe P.S. A. society, Warrington, Lanca- shire, desires to express its horror and detestation of the murderous deed by which the President of the United States hap perished at thelluatids of an assassin It repudiates with absolute bated all such sentiments as produce such deeds. It &wires Cr' express its pro- found sympathy with those who suffer in this calamity. the stricken and disconsolate widow, in her great physical infirmity and heart-reading grief, and the great people of the United States now called for the third time within living memory to mourn the violent death of their chosen President, Lincoln, Garfield, mtginley are names that will live in American history as those of men who faithfully and devotedly lived and died in the service of their country. May the God of all comfort uphold the mourning people, and may He speedily bring to an end all conditions and all doctrines which mar the peace of peoples and the welfare of the world." Two members of the brotherhood proposed and seconded the adoption of the resolution which was unanimously agreed to, all the members standing to signify their approysl. A copy of the resolution has since been sent to the American Ambassador in London. Rev J Yonge has rece veal the following telegram in reply :—"I thank you and the members of the W vcliffe Pleasant Sunday Afternoon Society, Warrington, fnr expressions of sympathy cmiveyed in their reso'utruin.— Choate, AMb issador."

WESTON POINT.

At the Wesleyan Men's Bible Class, beldson Sunday, the leader, Mr H Dodd, referred to the death of the President arid said that what impressed him was the consistent Christian charac:er he had always exhibited. From his early days to the end of his cireer be never forgot he was a Christian. Be commenced by being a Sunday school teacher and ended with being a Sunday school sup-rintendent, and though holding the highest position possible for him to attain as President of the United S:ates he was at the same time a humble wor- shipper in one of their Methodist samtuaries. While the world at large deplored the cutting off of so noble a life they, as Methodists, mourned the death of a dear brother.

A GENEROUS WARRINGTON EMPLOYER. Handsome Gift by Mr. Fletcher.

The following is a bopy of a circular which has been addressed to the employes of Fletcher, Russell and Co., Ltd.:— It will be remembered be all the older employes that on our silver wedding day I invited all, with their wives and families, to an excursion, and on that occasion, which happened also to b the Queen's Jubilee, I pre- sented them with a new Jubilee shilling, many of which, I am pleased to note, are still preserved as watch pen- dants, k;:. The sum then expended was £5OO. We have grown since, and at Xmas next, as a memorial of my 30th year in connection with the gas industry, I shall have the pleasure of presenting the employes with the sum of £l,OOO in cash, to leg divided as specified. On the occasion of our silver wedding the employes made us a present ; may I ask, as s pernonat favour, that they will not repeat this, as I am quite aware of their good feeling without any demonstration, and Ido not desire that any money shall be spent in return. In my 30 years' experience I have met with nothing but the kindliest .feeling and the greatest possible help from everyone.not only when I was alone, but also up to the present time, and I take this oppor- tunity of expressing my thanks for their help to every- one, without exception, with whom I have been con- nected during the last 30 years. The menet will be divided as follows—£so privately arnonese a few for special reasons and services. £250 amongst for men, heads of departments, travellers, and under-foremen who have, by their own efforts, worked themselves into positions of trust, and who are responsible for work- men in their charge, according to their length of service ; one share for each completed year up to Xmas, 1901. £7OO amengst al; not included in the above, in the same manner and proportions, one share for each completed year of service. Youths under 21 years of age to have half shares. To prevent cis- appointment, it must be remembered that £7OO divided amongst 1,100 men will not leave a large aineunt each, but the oldeet hands,with whom I have been personally connected for many years, both at Warring on, Pendle• ton and London, will receive the larger benefit. None will participate who entered the service of the company after Xmas last. No men will receive leas than 53, and no youth less than 23. 6!. in any case. All in Warrington Pendleton, Manchester, and London will participate equally ; no preference will be shown. roe amount will be pail in cash on the pay day before Xmas next. Any fractions out of the £7OO under sixpence, or odd surplus, will be paid into the Workmen's Sick Club, which will, of course, be of (goal benefit, as it will reauce the necessary subscriptions. The above applies only to those who are actually employed by Fletcher Ruesell and Co., Le., at the date of the distribution, not to any who may leave their employment before that date, whose shares will go to the Sielt Fund.—rnoetes FLETCHER, September 13th, 1901. Needless to say Mr Pletcher's generosity is much ap- preciated.

The last of the summer rambles in connection with this club was taken on Saturday,when Dale was visited, The party, wnich was led by Mr Madeley worked along the banks of the Wye towards Ghee Tor, There was not much insect life stirring, to the regret of the entomological section, though not to tha; of the rest of tne party. Lampy- ris nociiluca, the glow worm, was the most notable trophy in tne insect department. It was fairly common, and it seemed quite the fashion for these beetles to spend the day in snail shells. The gealogical features of the country were explained by Mr Madeley, an 4 the better to follow him the members heroically climbed up dizzy precipices, whence they could observe the various rock forma- ations and listen with literally breathless interest while he pbinted out the beauties of the landscape and explained how it had all happened. At Hobb's Hurst, Deepdale, a cave was inspected, which has recently been carefully explored and the various finds stored in the museum at guxton. From these it appears that the cave had been used in pre- hist3ric times as a dwelling, whilst during the Romano-British age it had been occupied as a shelter. The botanical sectioa of the party was exceptionally fortunate, securing many interesting and some rare plants. The weather was all that , could be desired, and the trip was thoroughly I enjoyed by all.

UONSCIENTIOUS 03JEC1ORS. Mr. Tanner Obtains an Exemption

Order.

At the Warrington Police Couit, on Tuesday. before Allerman Sutton (presiding) ad otner magistrates, Richard Tanner &gala made application br an order exempting his ctild from vaccination. It will be remembered that tits applicant fail( d to obtain an order last week. _ .

_ ...--.. In making the application Air Tanner said he did so from a conscientious standocint, as he believed Taccina- tion would be detrimental to the child. As he stated last week, he heard a paper read some years ago agaiust vaccination, and the proof 5745 so clear to his mind, that he was convinced that vaccination was detrimental to children. _ _ .

.. lhe Chairman You have heard that side of the question. Rave youever considered the good effects of vaccination _ .

Applicant said he had weighed up both sides of the quection PO far as he could hirnaelf, anl he had been waiting for two papers from medical gentlemen who were go,ng to present papers in favour of vaccination. They had not yet turned up, and he had come to the conclusion that vaccination wouhd be detrimental to his child. He should only be too pleased to hear the other side of the question. Dr Fox : We can't give you a 1-.cturp on it this morn- ing. Have you had any children vaccinated ? Applicant : I had one child vaccinated sn9l it is not now as healthy as the other three woo have not been vaccinated.

Alderman Shaw Green : From that you conclude that vaccination would be injurious to your child. Applicant replied in tne affirmative. . _ _ . .

The Chairma-n : I don't see how we can do anything but grant the order. Continuing he thought that it was rather strange. considering the thousands of cases in his experience where no injury had taken place after vaccination that the applicant should just put forward one or two cases be had heard of occasionally, where it was thought vaccination had caused the illness of chil- dren. However as that was the applicant's conscientious opinion they sranted the order. Dr Fos : We grant it because we have no alternative. " Didn't Believe in it." Application at the County Petty SessiOns.

At the Warrington County Petty Sion on on Wednes- day. Joseph Bradialry Woodhall made application for an exemption order. On being toked for the grounds of his ohjection to vaccination he replied " I don't believe in it.'

The Clerk : Can you give spy reasons ? Applicant : Ido not wish to state them. On being further queatinned applicant said he could not see that vaccination did children any good. The Chairman : Do you knnw of any children who have suffered in consequence ? Applicant replied in the affirmative. He added that he could not see that vaccination was of tiny value.. The Clerk : That is not a cnnsnientinua Would pm' sooner weir child died from small pox than run the risk of tieing vaccinated. Applicant : Yes. In face of this declaration the magistrates had no alternative but Co grant the application.

Warrlngton.—Advt The Volunteer Mounted Infantry Compan The members of the Mounted Infantry Company of the local Volunteer Battalion, spent a pleasant evening on Saturday, when the annual hot-pot supper and smoking concert took place at the Patten Arms Hotel. The company had paraded in the afternoon and been out to Hatton and Stretton. A."., the evening function there was a capital atten- dance of the members, and the colour-sergeants of all the companies were also present by invitation. The officers who attended were Major Rooms (adjutant), °await' C-nsfield (in command of the Mounted Infantry,) Ciptain Appleton. Captain Edelsten, Captain D Fairclough, Captain Smith and Lieutenants Avery, Ridgway and Williamson. Colour-Sergeant E A Connor presiled. It may be interesting to note that upon the formation of the company thirty names were received for member- ship. This number has been increased to BO when recruiting ceased four months ago. Since tha', time information has been received of the intention of the authorities to sanction an increase of strength , to 141, exclusive of transport. Recruiting will be again actively pursued,and it is to he hoped the com- pany will be brought up to the strength required. —Captain Crosfield in response to the toast of hie health, spoke in terms of warm apprelint;on of his men, and advised them to stick together and see that recruits were brought to the highest possible state of efficiency.—Major Boonie also referred to the efficiency of the company, and expressed an earnest desire that the wishes of the authorities might be anticipated by the strength being brought up to the required standard. He spoke in warm terms of praise of the hard work ungrudgingly done by the men, especially in camp, and said that although many of them were not accustomed to such rough and hard work they willingly Set about the taskond did it right well. STREET BETTING. Bookmakers Fined at Warrington.

The Chief Constable's Warning.

Four street betting prosecutions occupied the atten- tion ot the magistrates at the Warrington Police Court ron Monday. In the first case, William Parker, a young man, residing at 38. Ashton-street. was summoned for having on the 27th August and 11th September, and I other dates, frequented Winwick-street for betting pur— poses contrary to the bylaws. Defendant pleaded guilty. The Chief Constable said the defendant was one of the numerous bookmakers standing about near the cab stand at Central Station, and making a shield of the cabs:tock slips and sums of money from anybody—men, women, and children. The result was that a fat living was made on the foolishness of the lower labouring classes. 'the present defendant was the son of Thomas I Parker, the defendant in the next case and that case was aggravated by the fact that the father was bringing his son up to tai! nefarious calling. The defendant In the present case was watched from August 27th to Sep— , tember 11th by the police. On August 31st he was watched for an hour and a half and 33 bets were made and sums of money taken from working people. On septem her 3rd there were nine bets, snd on September 4th, 22 bets. On the 10:h there were six bets, including the net of a gentleman, he (the Chief Constable) had sent there to bet. Oa another occasion there were 27 bets. He could bring two cases against the defendant of bets made by a friend of the police, but he was not going to give him away. The defendant had been erect- taming the angel of the police unawares. The defend-- ant bad been t-peatedly warned. On Friday he (Mr Talbot) almost drove him away with blows; be had great difficulty in restraicing himself, as defendant was betting so boldly in Winwick-street. It was nothing short of a rublic scandal. The bookmakers had become so Impudent that if the police had not taken the matter up he should not be surprised if they petitioned the Cor— poration to put up stands for their convenience. The defendant was very short-sighted, and paid men to spot ' a policeman wherever he appeared. Several corner boys vetted up the crumbs from the rich men'a tables in this way.. _

- The Bench reserved their decision until they had heard the other cases.

Thomas Parker, father of the last witness. was then rged with a similar offence.

The Chief Constablo said the defendaat was one of the &del: arid most inveterate street book-makers in the town. Reused to be a docent cabman before be became a bookmaker. Oa Friday afternoon he (Mr Talbot) was pasoing down the street when he saw a youth 18 years of are go up to the defendant, and hand him something. He (the Chief Constable) asked for the slip and told the defeudant he had been netting again. The man hesitated, and be (Mr Talbot) replied that he would not have this street hotting, and that defendant would be proceeded against. "All right," was the reply, "I am very sorry." The slip taken frord the defendant and now produced referred to a horse that ran at the Doncaster meeting. 11 Harry Green, a youth living at 19 Greenall-street was the next defendant. He pleaded not guilty.

The Chief Constable said thia wag the atronerat case of the four. Defendant's father was a bookmaker, in fact the Green's were a family of bookmakers.
