Le .Roi est iitort:vive The New President. de Roi! is the cry of

the Republican demo- cracy, no less than that of the people under the monarchies of the world. It is the irony of fate, as represented by the American Constitution,that the successor to the late President of the United States is a mere nominee. COLONEL ROOSEVELT stepped into the Presidential chair as soon as it be- came vacant, ipse facto, just as KING EDWARD succeeded his Royal mother. He will occupy the chair for about three and a-half years, in a country that boasts of the necessity of a democratic elec- tion of its Chief Ruler every fourth year. 119 occupies his position untrammelled by " ticket or "platform," and free to carry out whatever policy he may consider it wise to adopt. So much are men the sport of circumstances, and the irony of the position is enhanced by the fact that this curious anomaly has been brought into &feet by the hand of the assassin. But PRESIDENT BooszvELT is not unknown to the American people. By the excellent process by which men of probity and sterling merit make their way to the front in the Republic, by dint of good service, he is practically as much the nominee of the American people as was his predecessor. Afe was, and is, a popnlar hero; and, as a matter of fact, was very nearly being run in opposition to Mr. MCKINLEY as a candidate for the Presidency at the last election. The reins of State may therefore be leik safely in his hands, and what- ever variations he may introduce into what was believed to be the intended policy of the late President, there is a strong chance in favour of its being in the direction of benefit to his country. He is the youngest President the United States has ev.er had, being but forty-seven yeArs of age ; but he has a record of work' accomplished which might have sufficed for many an older man. He is described as a pugnaoious Jingo. But this des- cription is scarcely fair or accurate. He is pug- nacious only in the sense of being independent in his judgment ; the determined enemy of wire pullers, and possessed of the strong, force- ful character which indicates the natural leader of men. It will strike Britishers as a little curious that a man bearing a name so unmistakably Dutch should be charged with Jingoism ; but it will probably be found that his policy amounts to that " same Imperialism " which is coming into vogue now, and is really what is essential in the Government of every im- portant State. Some Continental critics brand him as a " Tammany tool" ; but nothing could be further from the truth. Two years of the best service he rendered to his country were spent in a splendid effort to purify police administration in the State of New York. Previously, from the very ontset of his career, he had been a determin ed opponent of Municipal corruption, against 1 which he made a consistent fight for fifteen years, and heis the ; one opponent who is feared by the Tammany ring, who are probably the authors of the stories about his pugnacity. He is a spldier indeed. But he adopted the profession under the stress of circumstances and at the call of duty. It was indeed a call for which his strong, well- developed physique, and excellent animal spirits well fitted him. But he is a man of culture, eilu- cation and literary refinement. His wars have been wars against dishonesty,tyranny,and injustice. If he carries the same admirable qualities into the fulfilment of the new duties of his high office, as he has shown in other departments of public life, his country has nothing to fear from him; neither has the cause of civilisation and just government in the world at large.

The 39321351173 . The LateKinley President has eventually done Mc. its work, and WIILIA-Nt MCKINLEN has passed to the higher life. His last words will be memorable, breathing the spirit of resignatien in beautiful puritan simplicity : "It is God's way. His will be done." _lt was gi fitting good-bye, as worthy of a fallen Caesar as of the head of a great Commonwealth. It recalls the youthful training in his father's home, for he inherited the spiritual qualities of the Scottish Covenanter. Like the most of America's Presi- dents, MolcaNLay had known much struggle hab his early days. His father, though an iron - master, was a pioneer of the industrY7 and had much to contend against in the struggle for existence. Like the typical Scots- man he was, he had an ambition to give his son a good education ;. and when the younger McKINLEv showed a talent for the study of law it. , became apparent that he would make a career for himself. Previous to his coming to the Presiden- tial chair—for which he was in his second term- he was chiefly known as the leader of the Prot tionist policy, with which the Republican party i the States identified itself. Brought up in th atmosphere of the capitalists his sympathies were naturally with the manufacturing class, and his political economy was based upon the necessity of developing native industries in a. new country. Recollecting how his father had been handicapped in the beginning of an industry by the superior advantages of old-established concerns in older countries, his logical mind led him to the vieW that the only chance of developing home industries in America was to be obtained by giving equalis- ing advantages to home manufacturers. True,these countervailing duties entailed a sacrifice on the part of the purchasing public; but he persuaded himself—and for the most part he succeeded in persuading others—that this sacrifice would be made good in the form of increased wages which meant increased purchasing power for the , people. Perhaps he did not see far enough, or, if he did, he had no objection to the apparent fact that the greater part of the advantage of a high import tariff found its way to the employer, while , high wages attracted workmen from all the nations of the world; so that. in dealing with the labour market the capitalists had it practically their owe way. This experience acted as an intermittent check on the high tariff policy until the Free Sil- ver policy brought forward by BRYAN landed MCKINLEY on the crest of the wave as a champion of a gold standard. During the President's regime his policy had full play, and the result,frote the capitalists' n&int of view, has been a prodig ions success. Such success, however, has a tend- ency to bring its own nemesis. Between the tyranny of Trusts, which it fostered, and the misery and poverty of workmen, whom it did not k, help, a strong spirit of antagonism had arisen , throughout the country; and Mr. McKirmxY, his last public appearances showed that the time had come for a reconsideration of the position. What would have resulted it is difficult to conjec- ture, for the solution has been left to other hands.-

Leaders of the iron industry will be greatlY interested in an experiment which is being mad° in Norway with a process of extraction devised by Edison. The famous American inventor, who has already laid not one but all branches of commerce under a deep obligation, has discovered that mag- netism could be employed with advantage in the treatment of ores,, particularly those of lower grades; and the retult is the invention of " eziag,.; netic separators," successive sets of which used in this process to extract the iron 114.1"1,7" tidies. The trial will be made by a Britscompany, which has completed a contract W",lif the Norwegian Government for the working 0, the Dunderland iron deposits, and in two years time the preparations will, it is understood, b? complete. The Swedish ironmasters, we iire tolck are sceptical as to the success of the undertaking but of what new processes have rivals who follow older methods prophesied anything but evil ? The issue, it may be said, is of special interest to this country, not only because of the possible applic- ability of the process to our own industry, also on account of the, possibility, of the working of low grade ores bringing in a cheap and valu- able supply of rnetaLto British ironworkers. It may also have a favourable effect upon Swedish trade, which the latest Consular reports shew to be sadly in need of stimulation from some source or other. Commerce in Norway and Swede° suffers greatly at the present moment in gym' pathy with that of Germany, and the iron trade i 5 mentioned as one in which the severest depression exists.

will stand as one of the most ungracious acts perpetrated by the Town Council of Warring- lone know better than the members of that lei' what work the Mayor has done and how lently he has done it during the years he has occupied the position Chief Magistrate of the Borough. Those two have been years of an exceptional arduous dire both locally and nationally, and the position Mayor therefore has be3n anything but a iecure. Time after time Alderman Roberts has, this period, proved himself the right man in the it place. The active part he took in doing our to the volunteers and militia, in organising Relief Fund—to mention one or two things— always be remembered in his favour. Con- able progress has been made in local improve- Meats also during these two years, and much of it is due to the industry and energy of the Mayor. All things considered the least the Council could honourably do was to ask Mr Roberts to be Mayor for a third year,as was done in the case of his prede- cessors. However, another course has been taken, the expediency and fairness of which we must seriously question. Mr. Parr is a most estimable gentleman,and is much respected locally.He would doubtless make a good Maynr' with the somewhat important reservation that he has absolutely no acquaintance with the work of the Corporation 4/14:1 the needs of the town. Ho has not yet given r ais reply to the request of the Council asking him to allow himself. to be nominated and we doubt not that Mr Parr's well-known sense of fairness and gentlemanly tact will ecalle him to come to decision which will not operate unjustly towards Alderman Roberts.

MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

--- Reports from all parts of the provinces on Monday morning showed how remarkably the national pulse had been affected by the news of Saturday, to the effect that President MTinley had died of the wounds inflicted by the assassin. Pulpit references to the dreadful occurrence appeared to have been almost universal, and flags were said to be floating from public buildings in every town, village, and hamlet. Yet these anarchists live,move, tid seem to enjoy good living everywhere The Manner of the late President's death seemed, if Possible, to have increased the general feeling of rlttipathy. Not swift or sudden was the work of t,he murderer. Inasmuch as tbe cause of death was ,rlnown by the autopsy and the physicians' report to ,% blood-poisoning, presumably from the bullet lett 'n the body, it is clear that the President's fate was haled when Czolgosz tired the shot. Thus the Patient lingered within the shadow of death, full of Pain, a whole week, now buoyed up with hopes of recovery and then made conscious of approaching doom. Proud of her husband, his wisdom, and the respect he had inspired, he was something more to Mrs Wliinley than a partner in joy and sorrow. She had herself but just recovered from a serious illness, and begun to hope for the best, when he sank away with words of Christian resignation and true faith on his lips. Her sorrow must indeed be great, and all the sympathy which the highest and the most humble in the land extend to her in the darkest hoar of her life can bring but small corn- Ott to one so sorely distressed.

Once again the need has been demonstrated of a ! poison bottle which shall effectually declare the ; nature of its contents to the holder's sight or touch :1 To the lack of such a bottle the life of Professor . T.Yndall was sacrificed, and now another valuable life has been closed in the same way and from the I, aame cause. Mr Herbert Lloyd, of Birmingham, . Was suffering from quinsy and the doctor supplied , him with two bottles of medicine, while a bottle of . carbolic acid, for the purpose of inhaling, was ob- tained from a chemist. Apparently there was noth- Itg in the shape or colour of the bottles to distin- . gnieh one from the others, and the terrible mistake Was made by Mrs Lloyd of taking the carbolic acid .11P8tairs and administering a dose to her husband, zrom the effects of which be died, It is urgently equired in the interest of the public that all Poisons in addition to being labelled should be aupplied only in bottles of a particular shape which Would dely the possibility of a mistake. The best form hitherto recommended for the purpose, prob- ably, is that made of dark blue glass with corru- gated sides, in such shape as to be incapable of being placed in any other position than on its side. A few chemists have already taken steps towards "PPlYing;this nquirement,but mattershave reached Wien a stage of late as to make it very dssirable that the use of mese bottles, or something similar, should be compulsory made a kind of stipulation to the poison being supplied.

One has often felt inclined to question the sincer- ity of those persons who claim a monopoly of the desire to '• stop the war," and speeches delivered from many platforms during the present week have Certainly not tended to modify that feeling. A Pessimist correspondent at the seat of war in South Africa sent a telegram to the paper he represented in London, about the time Lord Kirchener's pro- cleMation was issued, saying that a general sur- render was espected on the 15th September as the result of the edict, and many persons accepted this Its prophetic and something the Government were relying upon. There has been no evidence that the latter was the case, and people generally expected to more than what has happened. But these " stop the war" orators have appeared almost frantic Oyer the reports from pro-Boer sources that one two, or three at most, have surrendered as the re- eUlt of the proclamation. When they opened their aperri on Wednesday morning, they would see from tile weekly report of Lord Kitchener that there had I.gen one hundred and thirty-three surrenders with. '2l the seven days. But what the public must want In know is, if these people really desire to stop the "war, why they are so ready to gloat over the few- tlees of the serrenders, according to their own re- Ports? The British public would te as stupid as they themselves imagine, if they did not see the I._`ltter shallowness and hypocrisy of the " stop-the- war " cry. All the week, since the 15th, these Professing i• peacemakers" have been openly glorying in the prospect of the war going on, despite every effort on the part of the authorities ,0 bring it to an end. The hollowness of their pre- tensions is too evident to be mistaken, and is Proved out of their own mouths.

----- Technical instruction is a very elastic phrase, as tertain of our local authorities long since taught 11; • But few people would ever have thought that 't could be stretched to the extent of meetinz the quirements of a member of the Newmarket Urban uistrict Council,who has just given notice to move, St the next meeting of that authority, that "owing ,t() the influx of American jockeys, the Technical ',clucation Committee be requested to secure the 'rvices of a competent riding instructor to instruct aWmarket stable lads how to ride properly." After recovering one's breath and taking time to thi ok, it does really appear that the ability to ride !111 the f million made famous by Sloan and 15 compatriots is necessary to English jockeys rrierally, and perhaps those of Newmarket particular. Their instruction, too, in the se- ence uf sitting on a horse's neck instead 41 its back, must be admittedly technical, though it may be doubted whether the training stable lads is precisely one of the objects for !hich Parliament consciously provided public neY. Be that as it may, the Newmarket idea aY be expected to bear fruit in other parts of the loat?lish provinces. Budding footballers may have 'le provided with special instruction how to • goals, and embryo cricketers bow to defend • • eti or deliver balie. Anyhow, other Technical !trllZtion Committees may be asked to make llnet observation of bow the idea works at New- ularket.

t 4'hile on the subject of Tort. it may be as well Make note of the fact that the first race for the raerica ZC has been postponed from to-day to at" ,Thursday and the second race to this day 14:84. "Wind and weather permitting," the reader pia'4l' well ejaculate in recollection of the experi- slite:a Of two years since. There is no doubt that ttl;4l3lrOck II has made a great sensation on the er side of the Atlantic. American betting is ts3r,t.rtearly so aggressive in its designs upon English te.!eliam as it was. If the race had been run tht-aY the boats would have started at little more " he-1: ".verlP, so quickly has Shamrock II established o4jfe,t as a dangerous competitor. It geeing practi- tiotY certain that if Sir Thomas Lipton's boat is .thi. Capable of bringing the Cup back to vet: country, it will remain in America a ikttiY long time to come. The challenger la ways of course, to be built strong enough to

cross the Atlantic, while the defender is finished for the race only. But, whatever else may happen, it is quite certain that the race will be followed with the greatest and most absorbing interest on both sides of the Atlantic,and while all readers will hope that Sir Thomas Lipton will have the pleasure of beating his old enemy, yet they will also wish that the better boat may win—and that it may prove to be Shamrock 11.

The annual report prepared by the Board of Trade for the year ended with March last, is not altogether satisfactory reading. It covers a period marked by a considerable amount of prosperity, but towards the end of the year 1900, there was un- mistakable evidence that the climax had been reached, and that trade was on the decline. The period under review was, on the whole, a fairly good one for making comparison and drawing con- clusions. There was a substantial increase in the amount lost to creditors, both in bankruptcies and private arrangements. Taking the forms of failure together, the accounts showed that the liabilities exceeded the assets by nearly eight millions and a half. The building trade figured unenviably at the head of the list of insolvencies, and it may be a surprise to some to hear that the beer, wine and spirit trade came the next. Farmers, in spite of the " usual " bad times, pay their way so well that, as a class, they compare very favourably with other great industries.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

SUDDEN DEATH OF AN OLD MAN.

The sudden death of John Capper. silk weaver, of Macclesfield, has been reported to the Runcorn coroner. Capper, who was 89 years of age had for some years lived with his son, who is a butcher in Egerton-street, Runcorn. Deceased went to bed at 8 p.m. complaining of not feeling well. At mid- night he was found dead in bed. The coroner deems an inquest unnecessary.

THE DROUGHT AND THE BRIDGEWATER CANAL. The long-continued dry weather has had a marked effect upon the Bridgewater Canal. The canal is about a foot below its normal level, and as a consequence the flats and narrow boats are un- able to take their full loads. In some cases the reduction is fifteen tons, and in practically all other instances the craft are loaded ten tons below their carrying capacity. This is not only a big loss to the traders, 'out also to the Ship Canal Company. PRESENTATION TO A MERSEY MISSION WonkEn• At the aoove mission on Monday evening a very interesting presentation was made to Mr J C Griffiths a worker associated with the place. The pro ceedings took the form of a supper after which Mr W Shaw presented him with a travelling trunk and later on in the evening Mrs Mack handed to Mr Griffiths a Sankey's hymn book. Mr Griffiths who is going away to Greenland set sail on Thursday in the Oceanic. THE FREE CHURCH COUNCIL.—MEXIORIAL SERVICE AT ST. PAUL'S.

In connection with the Free Church Council a memorial service for Mr McKinley was held on Thursday evening at St. Paui's. There wer.4 repre- sentatives of all the Nonconformist Churches in the town. The Rev J Osborne, B.A. delivered a suitable oration on the de,parted President. FIRE AT RUNCORN.

On Monday morning, an alarm of tire was sounded at Runcorn. The tire proved to be on a haystack, in a field belonging to Mr Roberts, farmer, off Holloway-road. Toe fire brigade, under the direc- tion of Captain Wright was soon on the scene, and after considerable trouble the fire was extinguished though not before nearly the whole of the hay had been consumed. The cause is not yet known, but we understand that the damage was covered by nsurance.

HOPE HALL Airssrox RUNCORN.

In connection with the above mission a very interesting social was held on Wednesday evening. Mr A Jones presided. The Weston Point band were responsible for the entertainment and were very successful in the marches entitled "Drum add Trumpet," and "For the Queen." "Roses under- neath the snow," and "Little sister's gone to sleep," were nicely given by Mrs Gatley Mr Rankin with " Only to see her face again " re- ceived quite an ovation, and Mr Brown with his humorous selections created quite a sensation- Miss 14 Jones' violin solo "The Vicar of Bray "was much appreciated. Mrs Brown and Messrs Pearson, Robinson, Blower, G Pye, J Furniyal, and Miss Dutton contributed materially to the success of the entertainment with several interesting songs. Mr T Byfield's selections on the gramophone were splendidly given and pleased everyone. On the proposition of Mr Strange a vote of thanks was passed to the Weston Point Band. PROMOTION FOR A RUNCORN POST OFFICE OFFICIAL.

Our readers will be pleased to learn that Mr J H Gleave, senior clerk at the Runcorn Post Office has been promoted to the Postmastership of Ellesmere, Shropshire. Mr (leave has tor a long time, stood high in the esteem of his townsmen owing to his pleasing and urbane disposition, and he will be missed by a good many persons who are in the habit of visiting the Runcorn Poet Office. Be left school in January 1871 and gaining an appointment at the Runcorn Pas Office has gradually worked his way, by dint o, strenuous exertion and faithful attention to duty to the honourable position which he now holds. At the time he entered upon his official career the Post Office was in Church-street. It was afterwards removed to High-street and subsequently to its present position. He takes up his new duties on the 7th October,and everyone will wish him "health and prosperity" in Ms new home. THE COTTAGE HOSPITAL FUND.

At the last half-yearly meeting of the Runcorn Co-operative Society the question of contributing to the Cottage Hospital scheme, which has been so nobly pioneered by the Runcorn Cycle Club was raised, the matter being eventually left over for a special meeting to decide. The adjourned meeting was held on Mon- day evening, Mr W E Dudley presiding. The Chairman read a letter from the Runcorn Urban Council with respect to the matter. A proposition being made by Mr J Taylor, seconded by Mr J J Bowyer,that the sum of £lOO be given. An amend- ment was made by Mr Fitzpatrick, Widnes, that the question be left over until the next half yearly meeting but this was lost, the resolution being sub- sequently carried. THE RUNCORN (JAS STUMM—IMPORTANT SALE. Yesterday afteriaoon an important sale by Messrs B and B Leach took place at Wilson's Hotel, Runcorn, and there was a large attendance. The free-hold residence, " Brookfield," Moughland-lane, Runcorn, was withdrawn at £950, the reserve being £l,OOO. Chief interest centred in a number of shares in the Runcorn and Frodshara Gas Com- panies. WELTON POINT MEN'S BIBLE CLLSS

At the above class on Sunday, Mr S Coventry deliv sued a very interesting lecture on "The growth and in- fluence of Methodism." The speaker showed how Meth- odism had commenced in humble surroundings and from small beginnings amid ridicule and obloquy. At the present day, however the organisation was world wide and it had produced sons which were a credit and honour to the Society. Mr Coventry cOncluaed his lecture with a very pleasing sketch of the proceedings at the 011rumenical conference. PRIMITIVE METHODIST CHAPEL.--VISIT OF THE _ _ .

REV JAMES FLANAGAN. It will be seen from our advertisement columns that this successful evangelist and popular lecturer is to preach and lecture in the Greenway-road Methodist Chapel on Wednesday. It can truly be said that Mr Flanagan is one of the most wonder- ful men of the day. Starting life as a collier amid the most oppressive environment, be has gradually worked his way into being one of the most promin- ent of London orators by sheer force of intellectual ability. None who beard him a year ago will miss the opportunity of listening to his exposition of Divine Truth, and his entertaining descriptions of East London scenes and characters. His lectures are a marvellous combination of humour, pathos, eloquence and dramatic force, and those who wish to be entertained and instructed should not miss hearing this remarkable man. For further inform- ation see advt. SUDDEN DEATH OF A CHILD. On Friday evening at the Weaver Hotel, Weston Point,an inquest was held by Mr R Dobson,coroner, concerning the death of the infant son of Evan Evans captain of the narrow boat " George," lying in the Weston Point Basin. Mr Grounds was fore- man of the jury. Superintendent Ennion repre- sented the police. Evan Evans, said the child was born on Sunday morning and had not been chris- tened. On Thursday while his wife was attending the child he suddenly screamed. Witness went to Runcorn for a doctor and on his arrival in a hour's time the child was dead.—Dr Delaney said be was called by the last witness to see the child which was dead when he arrived. There were no marks of violence. The child had died in a convulsive fit which might have resulted from a chill caused by an open door. The jury returned a verdict in ac- cordance with the medical evidence.

===== 0002601_19010920_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN NOTES. NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.]

The thrill of horror which ran through the whole of the civilized world at the announcement that President Mainley had succumbed to the murderous bullet of the assassin vibrated on Satur- day morning in the breasts of the majority of the inhabitants of Runcorn. The sudden and unto- ward cutting off of so great and so good a man was tne all absorbing topic of interest, and natural in- dignation,which for the moment clouded all local questions, was freely expressed against the sect whose fundamental doctrine is, according to re- port, the" removal " of the chiefs of Government.

On Sunday, as will be seen from our columns, references was made at most of the churches in town, ad in one or two instances Handel's "Dead I March" was played. At the Police Court on Mon- day the chairman, Mr A R Norman alluded to the ' deceased statesman and expressed the hope that ;something would be done to prevent any repetition 'of such an occurrence. Before, however, anything can really be done in this direction it will have to be decided whether the assassination was the rash act of an individual or the organ'zed blow of a secret society. Even if the latter hypothesis prove to be the correct one the war of extermination against the Anarchists will have to be carried on very warily indeed for there is always the stubborn historical fact to be confionted that the growth of a society especially a secret one—is accelerated rather than retarded by persecution.

The other day I had the somewhat doubtfnl pleasure of speaking to a Liverpool gentleman who knows something more about Anarchy in Liverpool and elsewhere than does the ordinary" man in the street." Himself a political exile from Russia, and , being an exceptionally clever man who may or may not be in the " know" he deprecated strongly that the assassination was the work of the Anarchist body. The principles of Anarchy, he said, struck at the root of Government by the belief that every man should be a law unto himself, but instead of having recourse to violent means to attain the end they believed in the dis- semination of economic doctrines, and in the farther spread of knowledge. Everybody knows that the times are a trifle out of joint, but to substitute the capricious will of the mass for the present law and order would be to let chaos reign supreme. The constructive policy of the Diberat is far more states- manlike than the mad rush of the Anarchist, or even the stow-footed policy of Toryism.

()rice more the Runcorn youth has an opportunity of extending his acquaintance with the subjects which will make him a better citizen and which will equip him to fight the battle of life in his later years much more vigorously and successfully. The art, science, and commerciai classes have now com- menced their winter .session in earnest, and those who desire to pursue any particular branch of study can do so at the Institute undsr the guidance of some of the best possible teachers. The high result q previously attained by the evening classes are sufficient commendation to ensure their atten- tion from local students, and lovers of knowlEdgo cannot do better than deepen their understanding by placing themielvesin touch with a trained mind I cordially wish the classes the highest p3ssible success.

At this period of the, year tradesmen, and others who profit by the wages of the working man 'find things" to use a sock phrase, 'quiet.' Besides being between the seasons, which itself causes de- pression there is another reason that is usually overlooked by the ordinary individual. Just at this time the books of the town must be closed for the half-year and therefore all the rates, water rate, poor rate, district rate and street mOring accounts, must be collected for their different funds. The majority of the working class leave these compulsory payments right to the last minute and then out of the town's earnings some hundreds of pounds have to be abstracted and placed to the credit of the Imperial and Municipal revenues. This, to a certain extent, sudden with- drawal from circulation of 83 much of the town's revenue or rather the diverting of it int ) other channels seriously retards for the time being ordin- ary trade which accordingly takes some little time to recover. This explanation really seems to be the only possible account for the depression which has stolen over Runcorn* during the pest month. All hopes, however of reviving prosperity are now centred on the build- ing of the transporter bridge, which it is under- stool, will be commenced very shortly.

===== 0002601_19010920_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOTBALL.

Though Runcorn won on Saturday by the narrow margin of a try they did not at all do as well as their supporters anticipated. Owing to the absence of Houghton and Inglestiald, the team was not as strong as it might have been and this, perhaps, accounts for the looseness of the attack and de- fence of the home contingent. Be that as it may the exhibition was by no means an interesting one and the play cannot be describei as brilliant. For- tunately the defence of the Linnets was considera- bly better than their attacking qualities else it would have farad badly with Runcarn's hopes.

From first to last Leigh played a very strong game, and proved themselves no mean opponents. In fact during the last few minutes of the match it was were touch and go whether they would not manage to carry off the palm of victory. Tney were on the Runcorn line most tenaciously, and it was only by sheer hard work that Runcorn managed to keep their lines inviolate.

During the first half Leigh were the aggressors and the home backs Sad not a chance at all to show their sprinting qualities. The game opened out considerably in the latter part and some very nice play was seen on both sides. The game was a very even one throughout but there is a very strong im- pression that Runcorn should have romped away with their opponents. They did not at all play up to their form, and if they wish to bring honour on their town they will have to combine a little better than they did on Saturday.

F_RII)A Y SEPTEMBER 20 1901.

NOTES ON UAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL. By " Ovalist."

As a rule Warrington do not start the season well,but this time they have belied the prophets and " gone oil" with a really resounding bang. For them to have de- feated the Cup-holders and the Yorkshire League cham- pions in succeeding weeks is a surprising feat and one that augurs well for the club's prospects in the season's tourney. Most of us expected that " paid " would be put to the Batley account, bat that Bradford would also have to bow the knee and on their own ground too was more than even the most sanguine supporter of the Club oared hope.

"Alone we did it" might well bathe cry of the War- rington lads after Last Saturday's game. There is no doubt they were a proud and happy team during the iourney home. Considering that this marks the open- ing ot the third season since Bradford were defeated at home Warrington's performance is not to be made light of. It was: certainly not made light of at Bradford where the Bark Avenue folk were much Impressed with Warrington's evident superiority. Coming as it did after defeating the Yorkshire champions in the cup-ties, War- riogton's win had no semblance of a fluke in it. The Primrose and Blues have a liking for Yorkshire foemcn and seem:destined to carry further the havoc wrought among.the Tykes at the end of last season.

The superiority of the Lancashire clubs over their Yorkshire friends is most marked, and on Stturday every match between representativr s of the rival coun- ties ended in a signal victory for the upholders of the Red Rose. Yorkshire papers greatly deplore the exist- ing condition sl things, but hope for more encouraging results as the season grows older. The wet weather will be to the advantage of the Yorkshire clubs when it comes, for there is nothing like mud for bringing foot- ball teams down to one common level. Weight is an important: factor then, and whatever else they lack the Tykes are not short of weight. A writer in the " Leeds Mercury " on Monday tritely observed that " Yorkshire club officials have been satisfied with limbs

andfmuscle,whereas ha Lancashire i bae been limb ...d muscle plus brains, which have ben -successfully culti- vated."

Oldham, Broughton, Swinton, and Warrington all polished off their Yorkshire rivals on Saturday, but it is the doings of the Warrington lot in particular that I must confine myself to.

The team accomplished the journey to Bradford very comfortably, travelling per saloon from Manchester in an hour and twenty minutes. There was only one change from the selected fifteen, Fell being replaced by F Swift owing to the arbitrary ruliug of the working clause. He started on the Thursday and would have been working earlier if possible, but his permit was re- fused for the second time. The burley Askaml man seemed to feel his exclusion from the team rather keenly and did not at all enjoy the part of a spectator, which he was forced to fulfil. With Boardman off also odds seemed that Warrington would be overwhelmed forward but they did ever so well against the big Bradford thrutchers.

The start looked none too promising for the home team got the ball from the scrimmage repeatedly and Warrington had to defend desperately. They proved equal to the strain, however, and before long the front rank was contriving to obtain possession occasionally. Burgess and Allen made good Use of the ball when they got it and bo Warrington threequarter line gave their opponents a busy time. After the first quarter Warring- ton seemed to have got the measure of their foes and began to put pressure on. Combined play among the backs saw the Bradford line seriously naenaced• Good tackling kept Warrington out for a tine, but at last Burgess smartly charged Murgatroyd's kick and scored near the posts. Fish was unable to land a goal and in this respect his old skill seems to have deserted him, though only temporarily. I hope. At the interval Warrington were on good terms with themselves, far they possessed a three point `lead.

In the second half Bradford made mighty efforts to wipe off the deficiency., but met a defence as steady and Impenetrable as a British square. The Warrington men were not content to defend either, and by dint of speed and combination they made numerous inioads in the Bradford quarter. Burgess got injured for the second time in the match, and Isherwood wisely put bim in Fish's place for a time. This move non-pluseed the Bradford men who had been carefully watching the left wing and before they had tumbled to the ruse Fish and Isherwood had broken through and the latter gave the injured Burgess an easy journey over the line. Hallam made a good attempt at goal, but Warrington have yet to register their first two-pointer in the Northern League.

Before the close a hot attack on the WarrinV,on line was rewarded by a goal from the clever foot ot Cooper, but this was the last score. Warrington adopted wise tactics and kept the homo men at bay thougn the line bad same narrow escapes. Once it depended on the verdict of the referee but Mr Smith gave Warrington the benefit of what was probably only a doubt in the minds of the spectators.

Bradford were fairly beaten, and the slore of 6 point to 2 hardly represents the superiority of the winners The homeaters bad undoubtedly the best of the argu- ment in the scrimmages, and held their own at half- beck but everywhere else they were out-classed. The ' Warrington three quarters played a really superb game, and while they did not do so much passing and running as against Batley they gave a fine display both on the attack and the defence. Bradford were as fast bebiud individuillv but in combined movement they were unmistakably out paced every time. Hariis again I showed that he is developing into a class playvr and his strong dashes quite caught the eye of the Brad- ford judges. Burgess and Allen did well, but it is at , half-back where the team is rnost handicapped for want of a "professor." Hallam played a great game, in the rear and had a good deal to do. Ward on the other side was a little inferior. The Warrington forwards deserve praise too for the deter- mination they showed, and their dash In the open. The Bradford men were on one common level of mediocrity, Marsden, Cooplr, and Pollard in particular not playing up to their reputations. Their ideas of combination are somewhat old-fashioned, and a leaf will have to be taken from a Lancashire book before success follows.

The park Avenue Ground was in fine order, and the. accommodathn excellent. As a footbail arena it will take a lot of beating. There was a crowd of about 7.000, which was not bad at enhancel ptieeL

It is interesting to hear what the other Side. _says " The Bradford Observer " noter writes as follows . " Bradford lacked the general smartness and determina- tion of their Warrington rivals, and were defeated accordingly. Quicker at se'zing an opening the Wilders- pool lads fairly earned their victory, granting that both tries belonged to the lucky species. It was a very good game to watch, however, and the most bigoted Brad- I fordian could not honestly grudge the winners their triumph, The combination amongst Os visiting backs was excellent, Isherwood, Harris and Fish required a deal of stopping. Warrington were almost equally good at defence, their fielding of the ball being well•nigh per- fect. In scrimmage play they were less effective, but the heel-out was managed so well that it amply atoned for any little forward discrepancies. The Bradford back-play without being actually weak, was still some way removed from high c'ass. Both full backs d's.in- guished themselves in getting out of tight places, It was a great feather in Warrington's cap to repeat the cup-tie success over Bradford, and it formod a fitting sequel to the same team's easy win against Batley. There is now plenty on the slate for Bradford to wipe off, but we daresay the real effort will he reserved for the cup-lies (second round) on March 22ad.'

Angling.

The Competition to decide the Championship of the Warriogton Anglers' Association was decided at Acton Bridge on Saturday afternoon. Oct of 41 entitled to compete,33 put in an appearance. .The general secretary Mr P Knight, had made good arrangements,and prompt to time,three o'clock.the proceedings commenced.As the two hours allowed for thej contest progressed, it became apparent that little sport would be recorded, in addition to the east wind;which was blowiag, the canal was very weedy,and the latter fact militated against good catches being obtained. With a catch of only 6DI ldr, Mr George Spencer was declared the winner, thus taking the medal and the title of champion of the Association. The contest for the juvenile members was fished in the canal at Thelwall on Saturday afternoon, Mr H Bent being in charge. The result was as follows in the order named :—Masters W Wright, jun, who registered a catch of Boz; J Holden, R Spencer, H Moores and W Thomason.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Byerley—Brierley.

Oa Tuesday afternoon a pretty wedding was solemn- ized in Holy Trinity Church, Warrington, when the con- tracting parties were Miss Marie Brierley, daughter of Mrs Brierley, the White Hart Hotel, Sankey-street, and Mr Ebert J Byerley, son of Mr Robert Byerley (Sheffield), late collector of Inland Revenue at Warrington. The bride was given away by her uncle, Mr James Gleave, and was attired in a dress of white Dochesse satin, trimmed with embroidered chiffon, and her head was crowned with a a rr.ath of orange blossoms with tulle veil depending therefrom. A WARRINGTON CARTER'S SINGULAR DEATH'

Extraordinary Conduct of a

Labourer.

A singular case was investigated by the Deputy Coroner (Mr F A Jones) and • jury on Monday after- noon at the Warrington Police Station. The death of Hugh Yardley, • carter in the employ of Messrs Buckley and Co, who resided at 127, Mersey-street, formed the subject of the inquest. It appears that at laeven o'clock on Thursday, August 22nd, the deceased in company with kman named Jenkin- son was in charge of a .van loaded with furniture for Mrs Walton, Fearnhead. When they arrived at their destination, for some reason the,furni- tura was not taken out of the van. At 230 the fultow- log morning the deceased returned home, bleeding from a wound on the right buttock. He told his wife that he was reaching a horse cloth on the shafts when one of the horses moved and he fell to the ground. In doing so he came into contact with a broken bottle, which caused the injury. He was attended by Dr .Langdale, bu;:, was seised with lockjaw and died. At the inquest Mr Steel appeared for the widow, and Mr Browne repre• seated Messrs Buckley and Co. Frances Ann Yardley, wife of the deceased, supported this statement. Deceased was sober when be left home and also when he returned.

In answer to Mr btee!, witness said her husband also told her that another man set out with him for Feara head but left Liao somewhere on the way, and when the accident happened deceased was all alone. He said he fell into a ditch and was lying unconscious for some time. At length, he managed to get up and was practically dragged to Padgate b 7 the horses. Here he managed to get on one of the horse's backs and rode to Messrs Buckley's. When he got to the place no one was there and he had to put the horses in as best he could. Since the accident she had received no communication from the firm in whose employ deceased had been since before Christmas. James Jenkinson, general labourer, 85, Church-street, said that at 7 p.m, oa August 22aa he accompanied the deceased from Mill-street to Fearnhes.4. They called at two public houses on the way and had a pint of beer at each. On atrival at Mrs Walton's at 10 p.m. witness took some things out of the van and went into the house. Oa returning he could see nothing of the de- ceased. He waited there until 11.30 and then went home,leaviag the horses and van at Fearnhead.Deceased bad had some drink but was not drunk. _

kr Steel; What did you think had become of him ? Witness I could not say unless he had had a sleep somewhere. I told Mrs Waltga I was not going to un- load the van myself, I said I would come again first thing in the morning. Mr Steel : Did Mrs Walton refuse to have the furni turA because deceased was under the influence of drink. Witness : I did not hear her say so. Mr Steel: What did you think would become of the horses ?

Witness : I cannot say anything about that. Further questioned witness said he made no search for the deceased.

By Mr Browne : They were loading the furniture from a cottage in Mill-street from 2 to 7 o'clock. During that time they had two drinks at the Mason's Arms. They called at two public houses on the way to Fearnbead. When he found &ceased was miss- ing he did not tell Mrs Walton he bad gone to have a drink somewhere. Deceased was no; very drunk but they "could see he had had a drop." Mr Browne: Was he fit to unload the furniture. Witness: No he was not.

Be had a suppurating wound on the right buttook three inches deep, and was also suffering from lock jaw. He died on September 12th at 7 pm. Death was due to the injury. Dr Langdale attended the man at his residence previous to hie removal to the Infirmary. On behalf of Messrs Buckley Mr Browne said the firm I were exceedingly sorry that the accident had happened. DEATH FROM IMMERSION.

A Latchford Woman Falls Into the

Bridgewater Canal.

Mr S Brighouse, County Coroner, conducted an in quest at the Warrington Police Station on Thursday, relative to the death of Martha Ann Wylde, 57, Wash. lane. Latchford, who died in the Infirmary on Monday night, item the effects of immersion. At the outset, the Coroner remarked that the jury would have to rely entirely upon what the husband said in coming to a decision as to how the deceased came by her death. Itiappeared that on Monday evening :she and her husband were at the house of Mrs Surnby, 11, Leonard-streer, Stockton Heath. They set out for home at 9.25. According to the police statement they had had some drink but were not drunk. They were on very good terms and left arm in arm. They were going home by way of Twenty Steps Bridge along the banks of the BridgewateiCanal• The husband said that on getting a short distance past the locks he went on in front of his wife rind hearing a splash he turned back. Be saw deceased's bat dlating in the;we.ter and:heard deceased struggling. Ile dived in to rescue her but eventually they were both pulled out of the water by a boatman named Joseph Knight, and another man. I):ceaaed was taken to a neighbouring house and artificial respir- ation having been restorted to, Dr Parker was sent for, and ordered her removal to the Infirmary. She was taken to that institution by the police, and died at 11 45 the same night from the effects of the immersion. Annie Sumby, 11, Leonard-otreet, stated that when deceased and her husband left her house to go home they were on friendly terms. They bid both bad drink but were not drunk.

John Lowe, lockrnaster for th 3 Bridgewater Canal Company, and residing at Twenty Steps Bridge. said that at 9.30 on Monday night he was attending to his duties when he saw the deceased and her husband go down the steps to the canal bank. They were both under the influence of drink, anyone could see that. Shortly after wards he heard a shout that someone had fallen into the canal, and on going Co the bank saw the deceased in the water. With the assistance of a man named Knight he got her out and conveyed her to his house, where artificial respiration was applied. She was subsequently removed to the hfirmary. When witness came to the canal bank, dccsased's husband was stand- ing against the wall. He had been in the water, but Knight had got him out. Joseph Knight, 565, York-terrace, Latchford, said he was on the deck of a barge when he heard a shout. Be got deceased's husband out of the water with a boat hook, and stortly afterwards he and Lowe pulled the woman out.

Nurse Scott said te deceitaed was admitted to the Infirmary at 11 o'clock on filliaday night suffering from the effects of immersionz and died at midnight from heart failure.

- -- -- James Wylde, the huiband of the deceased, was then called. fie said deceased was sober but bad had some drink. Witness was also sober. _ -

The witness Lowe was recalled and asked by the Coroner if the public had any right to go along the canal hank. _

Lowe replied that they had not. Really they were tre3passing, and if they went that Way they diu so on their own responsibility. XiThe Jury returned a verdict of "Aenidentsl death" and said the husband was entirely trie from blame. The Coroner, addreping. WyldA, said he saw what an unpleasant and even dangerons position, taking too much drink led people into. Supposing there had been a quarrel between Wylds and his wite before they went I out, what a irsition he might have been placed in, be- cause there was no one present bat himself to tell the story. Toe moral of it was that he ought not to take drink.

AWoman's Sad Death.

An inquest was conducted at the Warrington Police Station on Monday, by Mr F A Jones (deputy coroner) relative to the death of Ellen Musgrave, a widow, aged 52, who resided at I, Jolley's Court, and died in the Infirmary on Mon- day evening. Annie Barnes, 1, Jolley's Court, said ' deceased lodged with her and had been in the habit of sleeping on a sofa in the kitchen. At 10.20 on Saturday night witness and her husband retired to rest leaving the deceased as usual, in the kitchen. She was then in her usual health and spirits. On going downstairs at eight o'clock on Sunday morning witness found the de- ceased lying unconscious on the back kitchen floor bleeding from a wound at the back of the head. Dr Thorpe was Pent for and ordered deceased's removal to the Infirmary, where she died the same day. Deceased bad for a number of years suffered from fits and had fallen down on several occasions when seized with them. A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned.

Ou Friday morning, Ed ward Livesey, 13. Prince-street was charged at the Warrington PAice C ourt. before Councillor Smethurst and other magistrates, with neglecting his two children on September 12:h and other dates—Mr Browne prosecuted:on behalf of the N.S.P.C.O and said the prisoner had plenty of work to go to if he liked, but be was always drinking. For the last seven weeks he had Riven his wile only 284, an average of 43 per week. His children were poorly clothed and verminous but the Inspector thought the vermin was doe to the fact that the woman had no change of clothing to put on the children. One child was very sickly, and the lice might have come on her for that reason.—Prisoner's wife was called and said her husband, in addition to being a drunkard, gambled and be gave her a shilling now and then when he won anything.—Mrs Bowden, a neighbour, said she had seen prisoner abuse his wife and children. The chil- dren often had to run out of the house owing to their father's conduct. Prisoner was a drunken scamp.—Superintendent Monaghan described the prisoner as a lazy wastrel. He was a very bad character indeed. His wife was a hard working woman —The Chief Constable said that was prisoner's sixth ap pearance.—Livesey was then charged with neglecting to provide reaaonable maintenance for hi a wife and chi dren whereby he caused his wife to leave him—Mrs Livesey said be left the prisoner on Monday owing to his neglect. She now asked for a separation order.— The Bench considered an, case was a bad one and sent prisoner to goal for four months with hard labour. His wife was granted a separation order with 103 per WeEk maintenance.

===== 0002601_19010920_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrington and the Early Temperance

Movement.

1 Mr Arthur Mounfield, of Warrington, editor e the " Independent Methodist," writes to th • *Standard ' as follows :—I have followed with intertst the letters to which you have given space upon the labours of "The Seven men of Preston " in connection with what is commonly regarded as the first temperance society. I should like to say, purely as a matter of history, that an earlier effort and total abstinence society had existence in con- nection with a church of the denomination to which I belong. In March, 1830, Warrington was the scene of an effon made by Mr G LI Birkitt, of Dublin, and Mr W Wood, of Manchester. Toey were members of the Society of Friends, and appear to have held friendly relations with the " Qiaker Methodists," a body formed in 1797 by the fusion of Methodists and Qoakers, and which subsequently became the exist- ing Independent Methodist denomination. Several days of effort failed to secure a room for the teach- ing of temperance principals ; but the meeting houses of the Quaker Methodists were opened to them, and in one of these at Stockton Heath the first society of which there is definite 'record was formed. It was founded upon the following total abstinence pledge, which is dated April 4, 1830 : " We, whose names are subscribed, believing that intemperance, with its attendant evils, is promoted by the prevailing opinions and practices of society with regard to toe use of intoxicating liquors, and th lt decided means are called for resolve to abstain cSieag thetticealnuse and practice of, and by all proper In from the use of inebriating liquors ourselves, and dissuadeea nto odtibsecrosunf rtoemnanu intemperance." I do not suggest that the members cf the societies which were subsequently formed in Warrington and distant can claim a place in the history of temperance equal to that deservedly given to the Seven lien of Preston, but the date of the pledge and the well-established account of the society give it priority in date, it not in in- fluence, to the Preston movement.

NORTHERN LEAGUE.

More Lancashire Successes. Warrington Win at Bradford.

The Warrington team had a very stiff task on hand last Saturday, when they journeyed to Bradford to engage last season's Yorkshire champions. A fine success over Batley the Saturday before, however, had given them plenty nf confidence, and the prospect of facing the heavy Bradfordians at Park Avenue did not dismay' them. The team left Warrington at 10 55, dined im Manchester, and reached Bradford comfortably at 1 sg. Fell was unfortunately unable to play, the severity of the working clause having caused the professional sub- committee to refuse his permit. F Swift filled the vacancy. On the Bradford side F Murgatroyd took the place of Hodgson at half. Splendid weather favoured the event, and some 7,000 spectators assembled on the fine ground at Park Avenue to see the team which gave Bradford their quietus in last season's cup-ties. Mr J H Smith (Widnes) was the referee and the teams lined out as follows :

Warrington : Hallam. full-back; Fish, Isherwood, Dickenson and Barris, three quarters; Burgess and Allen, half-backs ; Morrison, Emoldson, Scholtze, J Swift, F Swift, Eden, Cunningham and Jolley, forwards.

Bradford: Ward, full-baek; Pollard, Cooper, Heseftine and Sinton, three quarters; Marsden and F Murgatroyd, half-backs ' • Broadley, Grayson, B Smales, F Smales, Robertson, Newton, Turner and Johnson, forwards. Bradford won the toss, and Swift kicked off, Grayson returning to centre. A. scrimrhage ensuind, and Burgess was early penalised, but Bradford failed to improve upon it. Heseltine cleared trout a scrimmage, and run- ning well, passed to Cooper, who gave a running pass to Sinton, but the latter was forced into touch. Dickenson made his mark, and kicked well down the field, but Ward neatly took the hall and returned. Cooper made a brilliant attempt at goal from a penalty but the ball passed just outside the posts. Scrim- maging followed in the visitors' quarters, where War- rington were awarded a penalty, from which, however, they failed to derive *any material advantage. A mull by Marsden gave Isherwood an opportunity to smartly kick over the line. Fish followed up speedily but was just beaten by Ward, who kicked the ball dead. War- rington pressed hard on the home goal, and an effort by Cunningham to get over was frustrated only just on the line. Strong scrimmaging followed. At length, Cooper relieved with a good run, and pissed to Pollard, who was tackled at the centre. Heseltine spoiled an opportunity to score by a bacillus's to Sinton. Then War- rington came again and eventually Burgess scored a try after charging a kick by Murgatrovd. Fish failed to convert from an easy positioa. Half-time arrived with the scores at—Warrington, one try (three points) ; Bradford nil.

Broadly re-started, and Isherwood shortly afterwards made his mark. For some time the visitors were kept in their own half, but Fish at length came away to the centre with a fins run. Even play followed for some time- Isherwood picked up and ran, kicking well. Burgess, who a few minutes before had been momen- tarily incapacitated, followed up and scored a try far out, Hallam failing at goal. The game, which through- out had been fast, was now somewhat keen. Toe Bradford forwards worked hard, and pushed toeir opponents close to the goal-line. Some loose play followed, and from a scrimmage Marsden made a good attempt to get over, but was tackled in time. From a position directly in front of the posts Cooper kicked a penalty goal,for which he was cheered. The game was new more smartly contested than ever, each side doing the best they could to score. Harris for Warrington and Marsden for Bradford each got away with a rush, but were effectively tackled. A flue display of passing by Allen, Burgess, Dickenson, and Harris followed, but the last-named was tack'ed by Sinton after running past centre. From the scrimmage Marsden passed the ball to Cooper, who repassed to Pollard, the last-named player being rushed into touch. Cooper made a futile attempt at goal from a penalty, the ball going wide. Heseltine intercepted a pass rather neatly and made off, but Harris, who followed close on his heels caught him near the line and pushed him into touch. Loose play in the Warrington half ensued Sinton made a dash, but was tackled just on the line. Grayson got the ball over, but the reteree was against the try. Bradford renewed their efforts and it was only the cool defence of the Warrington backs which prevented a score. Hallam made one or two fine saves, and eventually Warrington cleared the line,forcing the hemesters back to centre. Jolley got his shoulder in- jured and left the field, time being called a few minutes later with Warrington winning a well-contested game by 2 tries (6 points) to Bradford one goal (2 points).

Runcorn v Leigh.

There was but a poor crowd at Ir well-lane on Satur- day when Runcorn entertained Leigh. The visitors came with their strongest team, but Runcorn made an important alteration Wilkinson taking the place of Houghton at back and Richardson filling Wilkinson's place. The weather was all that could be desired. Runcorn won the toss and Dunbavin started,Butter worth finding touch at centre. Scrummaging ensued and Molyneux had hard lines in a good drop at goal. Leigh got well into the Runcorn quarters owing to Jolley tailing to gather at the right moment. A free kick relieved. Judicious combination by the home contingent gave no little trouble to the visitors' defence, and a dangerous rush by the home forwards compelled Pearson to touch down. Leigh in turn became the aggressors, but Jolley stopped the ri.sh. A series of exchanges gave Runcorn a more commanding position Jolley and Faulkner losing the ball at the ; critical moment. Ganley made a splendid run and was grassed by Butterworth when within an ace of scoring. The game quickly became one of a ding-dong character, both sides being visited in turn. Dunbavin beat his man, awl passing to Ganley nearly effected a score,but I Jones brought him down when on the line. Leigh still pressed the home defence bard, Ganley again being conspicuous with a fine run along touch, being stopped by Butterworth when on the line. Half-time arrived with a blank sheet. Jolley placed his side rep7atedly on the aggressivelbut the defence was „fn. jonpa dashed boldly for the line but erratic kick- ing enabled the visitors to reeover. The back division of the visitors however began to combine splendidly and contested every foot of the ground stub- bornly. The Runcoru men had a long spell of attacking and obtained a penalty goal. They kept Leigh on the defensive up to the interval, Leigh being unable to get away. The home team led at the interval by two goals two tries to one goal; but at time Leigh A" had increased their lead to 17 points to four.

Widnes Rangers v. Runcorn Recs.

ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL.

Runcorn Parish Church v Frodsham

Church.

Played at Frodsham on Saturday. Price started for the visitors, the Frodsham forwards afterwards attack- ing strongly being beaten back by Burrows. A passing movement Was afterwards initiated by Linaker and Unsworth the latter player scoring for Frodsham with a well directed shot after twenty minutes play. Half time, Frodsham one koal, Runcorn Parish nil. In the opening exchanges Runcorn had the best of matters and had the goal at their mercy repeatedly. Time arrived with Runcorn Parish leading by 4 goals to Frodsham 1 goal. Weston v Mariners' Mission.

This match was played at Halton on the new ground of the latter team. We,t)n had slightly the best of the opening sxchanges, and after some smart play Davies for the Mariners shot over. Half-time arrived with the Mariners leading by a goal. Weston went off in grand style, and the home custodian had a very lively time in repelling rushes. Exciting play followed, but Weston could not find an opening, the Mariners winning by one goal to West on nil.

Tue Rovers started operations and quickly obtained the lead, but before the interval the vieitors got on equal terms , again. Combination.

Newton commenced their series of home matches on Saturday, when they received a visit from Birkenhead. Both teams were at full strength, and the few specta- tors who witnessed the match were treated to a right royal battle —The visitors won the toss and set Newton to face a slight breeze, Hill starting operations. The homesters quickly took up the attack, a brilliant shot from Billiugton being neatly tipped over the bar by Kelly. The ensuing corner was splendidly placed by Kenny, and the custodian bad to concede a second corner to clear his lines. Kenny put the ball behind at the second attempt, and from the goal kick the visitors paid a visit into the Newcon half. They were not allowed to become really dangerous however, Raynor very soon transferring the scene of operations. A pretty run by Billington was followed by a capital centre which Butler met and banged into the net, quite out of the reach of Kelly. Straight from the centre the visitors retaliated and a beautiful shot frond R Lloyd beat Horrocks all to pieces, the leather cannoning off the Cross bar into the net. Paty was fast and exciting up to the interval when each team had scored once. On returning Newton quicky obtained the lead Broster finding .the net, from a pass by Kenny. Butley shortly afterwards increased the homesters lead. Newton seemed content to rest on their laurels after this and as a consequence they narrowly escaped drop- lag a point. Birkenhead did nearly all the pressing and Todd managed to steer the ball past Horrocka whilst almost all the forwards had unsuccessful pops at goal, the final coming with Newton victorious by three goals to two.

" BUI NOT ME."

"SoMe people would have given up in degpair—but not me."

That's the spirit that bear,' troubles and wins battles. It made a Newfoundland B inks fiihermau stick to his boat and his oars for three clays and nights in a freezing gale, without food or drink, alone, drift 200 miles, crawl out on a rocky coast, and drag himself to a but where he got help. "'The other two men gave up and died," he said, "but not me, not me; I wanted to see my wife and children again." About eight years ago, Mrs. Esther Price, of Anchor Terrace, Taff's Well, South Wales, had a dangerous attack of rheumatic fever. With good luck and good care she pullei through, but the struggle left its marks on her. All fighters have scars to show.

"When the doctors (two of them working together) ceased their attendance, I was left to pick up my strength as best I could"; so the lady says. "My liver was sluggish, and a sluggish liver is like a big stove with no draught. Days and weeks went by and an an- xious time it was; tor instead of getting better I was getting worse. I felt it so, and others could see it." He. appetite failed her and without that where was strength to come from? She was almost as badly off as the fisherman in the story. Sometimes her heart palpi- tated and fluttered, and she was frigtitened at it. It made her think of people dropping down dead. The sweats too—cold, clammy sweats—why should they break out on her? Yes, and the pain at the bottom of her back—what made that? If it had only come and gone now and then!—But it fixed itself then; it stuck like a poor relation who has moved in to live with you. She couldn't lie on her back for it, and so lost rest and sleep. “Then," Mrs Price goes on to say in a letter she wrote on Febrilary Bth, 1901, "then, as if the trou- bles I already had were not enough, the ibteuicatics re- turned and heiAl me fast by the feet. I was truly an object for piy. There I sat in the armchair helpless as a fly in glue and dejected beyond telting: "One day—one of the days God seems to hold in Eis hand for us when hope is nearly dead—a little book came to Us by post. Having nothing else to do I read it. The topic was Mother Seigel's Syrup and what it cured. Two cases were like mine. I sent to Mr Evans' shop for a bottle. I used it but felt no more than a ' trifle better, perhaps hardly that.

"Seine people would have given un in despair—but not me. I kept on till I had taken 20 small bottles. " Then :got my reward. My appetite came back the swelling in my feet disappeared, the cold sweats came no more, or any pain. Yes, Mother Seigel's Syrup did all that, luau I ought to be willing to have others know it."

M;ss M Evans, Lain, L'anart h, Cardiganshire. Further awards will be announced in toe newspapers from week to week.

The Proposed New Line to Warrington. At the halt-yearly general meeting of the Lanca- i-hire. Derbyshire, and East Coast Railway, held at the Westmins..er Palace liotel, London, Mr Emer- son Bainbridge, chairman of the company, presid- ing, Mr. Stanley said it appeared to him that the company was run for the benefit of a few directors and the staff. The prospectus distinctly invited subscriptions to construct a line from Sutton-on- Sea tb Warrington. After nine years there was DO such line in existence. They had been told that tbev would receive 3 per cent interest on the money duiing the cmstruction of the line, but the direc- tors bad refused to pay this. It was a crying scandal that a company stmuld be c wried on in £OICh a way, and lie asked the shart-holders to turn the directors out and put practical besiness men in their place. This was seconded by Mr. Fl 03G. The report was carried with several dissentients.

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY, SEPTEMBER 20, 1901.

OPENING OF A MISSION.

AT WARRINGTON.

Sermon by the Bishop of Chester.

The population of the eastern end of S. James' Parish, Latchford, has considerably increased of late years and, in the opinion of the Vicar and church ofieers, necessity has arisen for a new mission to meet the wants of this growing district. Some time ago, a scheme for the erection of a mission was set on foot, and met with the hearty co-operation of the church workers and friends. Thanks to the will- ing assistance rendered, the new building is now quite completed, and last Thursday witnessed the opening services. The mission, which is dedicated to S. Hilda, is situated behind Mersey View, Knuts- ford-road, and is one of the largest missions in the neighbourhood. It is ,a wooden erection, and in addition to two vestries and a reading room con- tains accommodation for about 350 persons. The chancel at the east end is well-proportioned, and admirably fitted and furnished. When the mission is used for purposes other than those of divine worship, this chancel will be screened off from the rest of the building. A large platform at the west end will then be found useful and convenient. The total amount expended on the land, building, fittings, etc., is up to the present £536 10,. Al-eady £386 has been raised, leavine a debt of about .Cl6O to be cleared of. Earnest appeal is muds for the liquidation of this debt. The first service in the new mission was held on Thursday last week and was largely attended. The Preacher was the Lord Bishop of Chester (the Eight Rev Dr Jayne) and most of the 10381 clergy were present. The service combeenced with the singing of the processional hymn " Blessed pity, heavenly Salem " and the other hymns sung were of a character befitting the occasion. The service was intoned by the Vicar (Rev F E Powell) and the lesson was read by the Rev R Greenall (Rector of frirappentiall). Special prayers on behalf of the new mission were offered. The BISIL,P based an earnest -diecourse on the words "Means of grace" which are taken from the general thanksgiving. Before speaking on the phrase itself his 'lordship alluded to the general thanksgiving and said that gratitude ought to be the great motive of their lives. Continuing, he referred to the expression "means of grace" in its broader meaning and said he thought they were quite justified in regardine God's teaching in nature as supplying a very real means of grace to their souls and lives. Nature was a book in which they might read and learn lessons about God the Creator Let them who had the guidance of the Christian faith by which to study the book of creation, learn to read abut God there. Let not those wonders which science was more and more bringing home to their minds end understanding come on dull eyes and dull hearts, but let them make them what God intended them to be—means of illumination of their seuls to enable them to learn more about the Father and the marvellous way in which Hehad worked His mysteries in the creation Speaking of the mere direct and special ways in which the means of grace had been prov ided, his lordship said they would fini them in the Christian Church—the body of Christ. The whole of the Christian Society was intended to be a vigorous,well harmonized body, of which Christ was the head,and through which the grace of Christ's spirit was flowing freely and influencing every part. Men, women and children should strive to be means of grace to one another. God had intended that they should be knit together in one living framework in orderto procrote the glory and purposes of God and the welfare of those am log wnom they lived. Literature, poetry, and science and all those gifts and faculties which God had be4owed upon men were powerful agencies, which, if they were puni fled and strengthened by the presence of God's grace, became indeed means of grace to others His lordship went on to speak of the special means of grace contained in the Sacraments. Continuing, he said they should be anxious to extend the means of grace to others As the population increased they should try, as they had done in that parish, to extend the means of grace to the new and as yet unprovided for population. He had heard with sincere gratitude and joy how many of them ba I helped in various ways to promote that goo i work. Ile had been told how some of them had given their evenings and spare time to help to decorate and furnish the building. These services, they could not help but honour and be thankful for. The building was calculated to verve in many ways, not only for the ministry of the word and sacraments,but it could be arranged in a differ- ent way for lectures and other things which would stimulate, recreate and educate those who were gathered together there. Then, they had a read• jog room and altogether the mission was a very valuable and many sided apparatus for extend- ing to those who dwelt in the neighbourhood, the means of grace. Might God abundantly bless that mission which they were to-day offering for His service and for the welfare of those who dwelt in the district. A collection was made on behalf of the building fun 1.

The Evening Service. Choral evensong was held in the evening, the Vicar again conducting the service. The first les- son was read by Mr W IT Stansfiel i and Mr E Gibson road the second. The preacher was the Rev W Binney, M.A, Vicar of Witton, North wich who took for his text "And if any man sey unto you, why do ye this? say ye that the Lord hath need of him: and straightway he will send him hither."Mark,ll.3. DurMg the course of his sermon, the rev. genileman referred to the useful purpose which would be fulfille I by the new mission, and spoke of the pleasure it. gave him to see the energy displayed by the parishioners of &James' in connec- tion with it.—lhe collection was again on behalf of the Building Fund. The collections for the day amounte I to nearly £l3.

THE MAGISTRATES AND THE "CONSCIENTIOUS OBJECTOR."

An Unsuccessful Applicant

The magistrates at ths Warrington Police Court on Thursday—last weak Messrs John IFAir- :-.lnrigh (presiding) land Mr viepherd—had before them an applicant for an exemp- tion from vaccination order. The appli- cant's name was Richard Tanner, who in formed the Bench that he had obtained an exemption certifi- cate for another of his children, and Abe was quite well. At a meeting he attended he was con- vinced in his own mind that vaccination was more detrimental to a child than non-vaccination. He had been waiting for something like nine years to bear a paper from two medical men in Warrington who had promised to present a pqper in favour of , vaccination, hut he had not beard it yet. Mr Hepherd • We clon't want you to make a speaoh. We want you to give your conscientious objection. The applicant said ha did not think vaccination would he beneficial to his child. The Chairman : What. expefience have you had ?! Applicant : I have s,en children, ard know that after vaccination they have be.:n.sufferqrs.

The Chairman; Not your own childrn Applicant: Not my own because they have not been vaccinated. .

Mr lieph rd: You have not convinced me that you hare a con,cientious objection. I object to signing the order. Rephord then left the Bench.. _ .

..... Thel Clerk said that Mr Faircicugh would be unable to grant the order, as two magistrates were necessary. The Chairman : I have a conscientious objection to signing these orders because I think every chi,d ought co he vaccinated. I have seen enough of small-pox in Warrington. However, I cannot sign the order. You will have to apply again.

Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company Limited.

The report to be presented at the 28th annual meeting of shareholders, to be held in Warrington on the 30:h inst . states that the profits for the year ended June 30, 1901, amounted to £142.424 12s 21, which with the balance brought for ward from last year (£8.146 4s 10d) gives a total of £150,570 18s. An interim dividend at the rate of 6 per cent per annum on the Preferencs shares and 10 per cent per annum on ths Ordinary shares was paid in March last f‘m• the balf-vear ended December 31, 1900, amounting to £37,625, and the directors now recommend that for the past half-par a dividend be paid at the rate ot 6 per cent per annum on the Preference, and a dividend a; the rate of 15 per cent per annum,free of income-tax,on the Ordinary shares, making, wita the intetim dividend, 12 per cent for the year, and a total distribtrion of £90,437 IN. The direct ors further recommend that £25,000 bs written off the colliery leaes and good- will account,end £25,000 added to the reserve fund, and that the balance £10.133 Sc, be carried forward A sum of £60,000 has been placed on special deposit on account of the reserve fund with Parr's Bank, Limited. The trade of the year opened satisfac- torily, but in the early autumn toe demand for iron and steel fell away, and prices declined. Later on the same influences adversely affected the coal trade, and at present the cutlook of busi- ness is uncertain.

Then the American paid £3 a ton for his coal in order that the directors of the company might procure titled men for sons-in-law. This fable teaches that there are almost as many ways of paying taxes as of dodging the same. - A VERY calculating young man has kept for two years an accurate account of every penny he has spent in theatre tickets, carriages, flowers and gloves in connection with each lady he has invited out, inclusive of chaperons. On the credit side of that day book of pleasure is recorded his estimates of the satisfaction the lady gave him in return for the money expended. This prudent man just turns to his account-book before inviting, and by comparing the record sees from which charmer he gets most for his money 1

A TEACHER, wishing to impress upon the minds of her pupils the full nature of faith, took them one day to the river-side, and, seeing a boat in the middle of the stream, said "Now, my little dears, if I were to tell you that there was a leg of mutton in that boat, would you believe me?" "Yes." "Well, then," she said, " that is faith." Seme time after the same children were going through an examination, when the question was asked, "What is faith?" and all the class, as with one voice, shouted out: "A leg of mutton in a boat."

HE stopped at the corner, gazed into the sky, scratched his chin, and pulled his whiskers. Then he fumbled ire his pocket, wrinkled his brow, pursed up his lips, and blew his nose in a manner more deliberate than the collector of a bad debt. Anon he opened his mouth, tugged at his lower lip, muttered to himself, and fixed his eyes on the chimney•tops in a vacant, stony stare, that soon collected a crowd, which blockaded the pavement. He was a married man, trying to think what it ware his wife had charged him to be sure and get.
