Ittttrs to tbe etittor.

Election of Mayor. Sir,—A movement has been started for the purpose of electing Mr J Charlton Parr to the civic chair in November next. On looking over the list of Mayors who have served the Borough during the last few years I find that Messrs J R Pickmere, J Harrison, E Greenall and J Fairclough served for three years in succession. Alderman J E Wright I think was asked to serve a third year but declined. Alderman Roberts has been Mayer for two years only. He is I understand willing to allow himself to be re-elected. Why such a strenuous effert should now be made to effect a change is to me passing st-ange. Alderman Roberts has fulfilled the duties of his cffiee with credit to himself and benefit to the Borough. He has been regular in his attendance at the meetings of the Council. He has shown a thorough knowledge of the business to be transacted. He hal expressed his cpinions with lucidity and candour. He has conducted himself with grace and dignity, and he has manifested the deepest interest in all move- ments that were likely to conduce to the Shysical, intellectual and moral welfare of the people. Comparisons we know are odious, but it is generally thought that no more efficient Mayor has ever presided over the deliberations of the Council. All who know Mr J Charlton Parr know him to be a gentleman of broad s)rnpathies and stainless honour. The hall which bears his name and the interest he! has taken In the Young Men's Christian Association and other kindred societies reflect credit upon bis head and heart but his most enthusiastic admirers would hesitate to say that he was conversant with matters of local government or took the slightest interest in municipal affairs " Honour" says the old adage "to whom honour is due." Just so, and if sr, then municipal honours oureht to be given only to people who do successful work. It was stated in the public prints a few days ago that a memorial asking Mr Parr to allow himself to he elected Mayor was being numerously signed. The facts are I presume as stated but it is an open secret that some gentlemen signed the document under the inqamsion that Alderman Roberts had intimated his unwillingness to take the position for a third year, and that others were influenced to sien by seeing their names. These facts detract somewhat from the value of the docu- ment. It is a curious fact that the names of the Free Chnrch miniatera of the town are not on the Memorial. How is this ? Is it because the canvaineers were unwillirg to call upon them ? Or is it because ,they refused to lend their names to a proceeding which did not commend itself to the approval of their judgment. The number of persons present at the caucus meeting which was held to consider the question of the Mayoralty for next year was 29, twelve voted in favour of asking Mr Parr to accgot the position: 9 were in favour of asking Alderman Boberts to serve for a third year. eight were neutral. It is not likely that Mr Parr will fe-I flattered by a request from t welva out of thirty. 1 have no authority to speak for the Free Church Ministers of the town and _

I will not presume to do so but I venture tn express the opinion that if Mr Parr accepts the invitation of the caucus and is elected Mayor he will have no more loyal supporters than the Free Church Ministers of the town hut some of us will continue to think that Alder- man Roberts ought to bare been cffered the position, Yours etc.. CHAS. FINCH, 31, Arpley-street.

Sir,—l was much amused to read a statement, in one of your contemporaries, emanating from Mr Edwin Woods, that the move to persuade Mr Parr to areent the position of Mayor, was not a political one. When the same paper gave a list of thme who had signed the petition on the matter, it showed at 'a glance that with very few exceptions indeed they were all con- nected with local Toryism, and I venture to say that Tory officials had been round to obtain the signatures. This is no spontaneous outburst of feeling, in favour of Mr Parr becoming Ctief Magistrate. With all due respect to this worthy gentle- man, I maintain that the present Mayor has been snubbed, and this after his valuable services to the town. The Caucus meeting ought not to have been held in his absence. (Were they afraid of his presence, Mr Editor?) In all fairness he ought to have been present, and if the majority of the Council desire a change, why not have acted fair and above board. As a ratepaver of the town. I don't believe in gentle- men being pitchforked into the Council as Mayor of the Borough whether they are Capitalists or not. Surely there are members of the corporate body fit to take the position, without going outside the ranks. If the Con- servatives are not ready with a man let them own up, and invite the present Mayor, who has done equal ser- vice, if not more thtn many of his predecessors to again take the position. Yours truly, WARRINGTONIAN.

FRODSHAM.

ALLEGED ATTEMPTED SUICIDE AT FRODSHAM.—At the Frodshain Police Court on Wednesday afternoon before Mr C Reynolds (in the chair), and J Murray, Laurence Bernard Cobb, a youth of about 21, was charged with attempting to commit suicide by taking poison on the 13th inst.—Mr Kennelly, chemist, deposed to selling a bottle of rat poison to the, prisoner who asked would it poison anybody.—Edward Jeffs, baker, Flelsby, stated that at 11 a.m., on the 13th inst he was coming along the Chester road when prisoner met him and went with witness down Smithy-lane. Prisoner stayed behind a little, and then shouted out that be bad taken poison. Witness turned round and saw him on the ground. Be St once raised an alarm and afterwards brought P.C. Bancreft.—Dr Bryon, Helsby, said he took prisoner to his surgery. The stomach pump was used but the washings from the stomach showed that he had not taken any poison at all.—P C Bancroft said that-On being called to the prisoner he adminiatered a strong emetic of salt and water. His lips were black with the poison, and he smelled strengly of phosphorous. A bottle half-full of poison was found on hlm.—Dr Bryn recalled,said the emetic would have made no difference. Had plsoner taken poison the washings would have smelt of .phosphormis.—Letters were also found on the prisoner in which he declared his intention to take away his life, but these were not read in court.—The Chair- man a h o auge practical id edJoke.—Every body's time had been wasted. There was no evidence to convicc,and nothing to show he had swallowed any poison. It would do prisoner good if they could give him 14 day, but they could not. —Prisoner was discharged.

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY, SEPTEMBER 20, 1901

LATEST NEWS.

Terrible Naval Disaster. _ British Destroyer Founders.

Sixty Lives Lost.

The mcst appalling disaster in regard to loss of life since the logs of H M S Victoria in the Mediter- ranean has occurred in the North Sea. The new torpedo boat destroyer Cobra has foundered on the Godgeon Bonk, about forty five miles from Yar- mouth, .4 and it is feared that about 60 men have been drowned. At eight o'clock yesterday (Thursday) morning the P and 0 steamer Harlington (Captain Young) arrived at Middles- borough with 12 survivors aboard. It appeps that; about six o'clock on Thursday night Chief- engineer James, of the Harlington, who was on the bridge with the captain at the helm, eav a boat several miles away, the occupants of which were frantically endeavouring to attract attentioe. Seen through a telescope the occupants of the boat eeemed to be in the greatest distress, and Captain Young immediately gave order; to steam in their direction. At about 6.13 the boat was cverhaule d and its comments taken aboard in a moot exhausted condition. The occupants of the boat numbered 12, and included Chief-engineer Pearsey, R,N., nine non-commissioned officers and men of the Royal Navy, and two mechanics from Messrs Parson's works on the Tyne. The officer in charge was almcst entirely without clothing, and he and all the men were in a sadly exhausted can. dition, having been battling all day with a high and dangerous sea. There had been no time to take aboard water or provisicn9 and all were suffering from hunger and thirst.

A Warrington Soldier's Gallantry Rewarded.

On Saturday several Humane Society's medals and certificates were presented at Preston by Colonel Murray, commanding the North-Eastern District. One was received by Private Richard Houghton, of the 4th King's Royal Lancaster Regiment, who Ryes at 49, Tilley-atreet, Warrington. The particular act for which Houghton received the modal is worthy of Epecial mention. On February Bth the regiment to which Houghton belongs, and also a Canadian regiment, were camped in the vicinity of Fish River, Cape Colony. Two of the Royal Lancasters and one Canadian !went for a bathe in the river. They soon got out of their depth, and tbe soldiers on the bank heard cries for help. These cries were repeated,and Houghton, without a moment's hesitation, p:unged into the water.rescuing the soldiers one by one. Unfortunately, the Canadian died after being brought ashore.

A Present for Warrington Volun- teers.

A beautifully-coloured commemoration card is to be presented to each of the 1.38 Warrington, St Helens,:and other Volunteers who have been on active service in South Africa. In the centre is an illustration of Table Bav and its numerous ships, while on the left when looking at the picture are the words "Belmont, Enslin, Mockler River, Kimberley, Paardoberg, Bloemfontein, Surrender Hill, and Wepener," and on the right band side are the words " Talana Hill, Elandslaogte, Pieter's Hill, Ladvemith, Johannesburg, Pretoria,Maebadodorp, and Mafeking." Below the view of the sea is the inscription : "To the cffieers, non-commis- sioned officers and men ELM, Volunteer forces of England. Scotland, Ireland, Wales, Australia, Canada, Cape Colony, Ceylon. Natal, New Zealand, Rhodesia, Tasmania." The following appears at the foot of the card and is signed by the Mayor of Cape- town :—" Capetown joins with the empire in feelings of heartfelt thanks for the signal services which EL M.'s citizen soldiers have rendered in upholding the Imperial authority in South Africa. The empire is ji:stly proud of the gallant conduct of the volunteer forces during the engagements throughout the campaign, and Capetown , congratulates the Volunteers upon the consummation i of a service which has resulted in the consolidation of the empire, and which has cemented the ties of affection between the colonies and the mother country. The citizens of Capetown wish you a prosperous voyage and a happy home-coming, where our .hearts will join in the rejoicing 97( homes which await , you. 6 God save , the Queen.' Octobq 29 .b, 1900.

VIADUCT INSTITUTE TECHNIC AL INSTRUCTION CLASSES.

From an advertisement on the front page it will be seen that the.e classes will open for the winter session on the 23rd just, and intending students should join without delay. As before, classes will be held in Science, Art. Commercial, and Domestic Economy. and an efficient teaching staff has been secured. The fees for any subject, except Domes- tic:Economy are 2s and for each additional subject Is, but tudeuts taking tour or more subjects will be charged 4s 6i only for the sB-ion. For Cookery, Laundry,Millinery and Needlework, the fee is 2s 61 for each subject. ST. PETER'S CHURCH BIBLE CLASS, NEWTON' The annual meeting of the members of this class was held on Monday evening. Mr T Wilkinson pre- sided and there was a good attendance of members, including the Rev James Ryder. In the election of officers, Mr Ryder was appointed president; Mr T Wilkinson, chairman ; Mr J 11 Lane, secretary; Mr Wilson, treasurer; and a committee consisting of eight members. A committee was also appointed to take into consideration the question of forming a gymnasium to b 3 held in St Peter's Schools. It was decided to have a stall in connection with the Bible class, at the forthcoming bazaar in aid of funds for the completion of the church tower, and one of the bells. _ GOOD TEMPLARY.

Hope of Earlestown Lodge meeting was held in the Co-operative Hall, Wargrave-road, on Monday evening. The Secretary read a post-card from Bro Walker, DT, accepting the invitation to deliver the address at the Anniversary tea. The programme for the evenirg, prepared by Sister Miss Woods, was opened by singing" Work for the night is coming"; song, " Strangers yet," Sister Williams ; reading. "Ben Seddou's courtship," Bro Warburton ; song, "I'll take you home again, Kathleen," Bro Hatton recitation, Sister Naylor; mouth organ solo, Bro Wilson ; song," Gates of the West," Sister Woods ; recitation, "The Quack Doctor," Bro Warburton; song, " I cannot sing the old songs," Sister Williams ; mouth organ solo, Bro Wilson. The Lodge was closed in the usual manner by Bro Williamson, C T. VULCAN WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL.

In connaction with the above, har-v—e?t-Thanks- giving services were held in the schoolroom on Sunday afternoon. The school, which is also used as a chapel, was very prettily decorated by Mr C Spottiswood, assistsd by Messrs Martin, Wadsworth, Ferguson, Mrs Devereux, Misses Platt, Bent and Astall. The decorations were in the form of three archways, the columns being composed of flowers and leaves, the top of the arch was covered with vine sprays from which bunches of grapes wer suspended. The text "God giveth the increase" wa handsomely done in blue and gold, with sheaves o corn in the centre. Tables laden with fruit, vege tables, flowers, &c, were placed in front and gave a very pleasing effect. Tile services were conduc- ted morning and evening by the Rev Moffatt Gaw- trey, who also preached a couple of excellent ser- mons. There were large congregations at each ser- vice, and the collections were quite satisfactory.

s SHOOTING AFFAIR AT Sr HELENS.'

Abolt twenty minu:es past ten on 111-o-i;d74.y night a shocking accident happened :at the L. & N W.R. Station St Helens, • when a man about twenty- seven years of age by some means got on to the line and was run over by a train corning in from Liverpoo/. Both the man's legs were cut off high up, and had afterwards to be amputated at the thigh by Dr Jackson. He was removed to the Providence Hospital, and stated that his name was John Boyle, and that he belonged to Black- burne. He had been working in St Helens three weeks, and lodged in Back Appleton-street. Peaslev Cress. ANARCHIST SCARE AT Sr. HELENS.

Mr F iJones, deputy coroner, held an-i—nouest at the Waggon Hotel, gaydock, on Tuesday, on the body of Thomas N wcross, aged thirty, 103, Clipsley-lane, Haydock. who died on Sunday morning in the Haydock Cottage Hospital. Mr Matthews, Government inspector, Mr E Peace, representing Messrs R Evans and Co., Mr Watkinson. certified manager, and Inspector Palmer were present. The deceased, said his brother, wits a day wageman, and had worked. at the Legh Pit for about eighteen years. The witnesa was the under manager of the coil cry, and was satisfied that his brother's death was an accident. The deceased was thoroughly familiar with all kinds of mining work. He held a aecond-class mining engineer's certificate.—James Hasiede, fireman, said that on Saturday night, about midnight, in Talbat's level of the mine, deceased was making a bole for a prop. Previous to this the deceased hsd tested tho roof, and it sounded somewhat bad, so that they decided to put up a bar. About five minutes afterwards a stone fell out of the roof on to the door and rolled on to deceased, who was kneeling at the time. The witness pulled the deceased from under the stone, which weighed between 4 and 5 cwts.—Mr Matthews said that there was a space of four feet between two bars, and in this space the men were setting a bar. They had previously propped a partly. broken bar, and in driving the wedgen in might have shaken the roof.—A verdict of " Accidental death" was returned.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT]. FOOTBALL.

Only two Saturdays have gone of the football season, vet thos3 two Saturdays have provided another evidence of the in and out form Earles- town invariably show. They open their season by visiting the latest recruits tu Lancashire League football and return defeated by two goals to nil, and last week they beardsd the League champions in their den and play them to a goalless draw. Almost the same team was responsible for these two practical illustrations of the uncertainty of football. The only difference from the team which was defeated at St Helens was that the forward line had been slightl3 juggled about; the rest of the team was identically the same. However, if the form shown at Statybridge is sustained, one thing is certain, Earlestown will be nearer the top of the laider when the reckoning up comes than they were at the close of last season.

Newton opened their ground on Saturday by entertaining Birkenhead and after a hard game managed to gain the verdict by the odd goal. The game was only a few minutes old when Butler placed Newton ahead, but the Cheshire team al- most immediately got on level terms again, a grand shot from R Lloyd beating Horrocks all the way. Both goals were subjected to severe pressure before the interval but the IV histl9 blew with the teams still level. Very shortly after the resumption, Brcster received a pass from Butler, and gave New- ton the lead whilst a few minutes later Butler increased the score to three, putting the ball into the net whilst the defenders were clustering round the referee claiming for offside. Birkenhead seemed to realise their position after this reverse as they keot up an almost continued pressure on the Newton goal. Florrocks had as lively a time as he is likely to have for some time, shots being rained in from all angles. Only one of these took effect however, a beautiful effort from the foot of Good entering the corner of the net quite out of reach of the custodian, and Newton gained a somewhat lucky victory by three goals to two.

Perhaps nobody was more surprised at the victory they gained over the Rovers on Saturday than Sutton Commercial themselves. The latter ere a comparatively new team, haying only joined the Liverpool Amateur League this season but they romped round the Rovers to the tune of three goals to one, and their victory was no fluke either. They were easily the better team. especiallY in 'the last ten minqtes when they played their opponents to a standstill. The Rovers will have to improve if they are to equfl last season's record.

THE LATE SERGEANT

INSTRUCTOR HANCOCK. Military Funeral at Newton.

The remains of the late Sergeant Instructor James Hancock, of the Newton Detachmont, 6th V.B. King'a Liverpool Regiment, were interred on Saturday after- noon, -with full military honours. The afternoen was very fine, and a large concourse of people lined the route of the funeral corl6ze, On parade were the Newton detachment of the 8:h V.B. (K.L.R ) and the battalion band, together with the local bugle band, under the command of Captain Parker of Liverpool ; number of men of the " G Company, Ist South Lancashire , Volunteers under the command of Colour Sergeant G Bailey, and the " C " Squadron of L incashire Hussars. ' imperial Yeomanry, under the command of Sergeant M4jor Pears, the whole being in command of Major Rowe, in the uniform of the Rosa! Marie' Light Infan- try, adjutant to the 6-h V.B K.L.R. Toe carriers were all sergeants of the 6th V.B. viz., Sergeants Wright, Rose, F Appleton, W Kent, R Hill, and H jsiobbs. The roffin was borne by these from the deceasrd's late residence to the hearse. A firing party of 13 privates and a Lance. Sergeant under the cntnmand of Sergeant Rinamer, preceded the hearse, with arms reversed, and immedi- ately behind the band, which played the "Dead March" ea ?vote. Behind the hearse came a carriage containing the deceased's relatives followed by the Newten de tacbment 6th V B, G Company, Ist S L and the Hussars in their new khaki uniform, with slouch hat and cock- ade of black feathers. On arrival at the Cemetery, th 3 coffin was met by the Rev James Roder, Vicar of St , Peter's,Newton, who conducted the service. The coffin, ' which was covered with a new Union Jack, surmounted by the deceased's belrnit, sash and s a ord was then con- veved to its last resting place, the pall bearers being Quarter Master Strange, Sergeant Major Abbott, (Regular) Quarter Master Sergeant Floltrate (Lancashire Hussars) Colour Sergeant Bailey, Ist VB,S L, Colour Sergeant T Appleton and Colour Sergeant J Morris (Liverpool) 6:13 VB, B L R. When the coffin bad been lowered into the grave three volleys were fired, and the bugle sounded the last post amid many expressions of sympathy. The military aftbrwards departed, the band playing a lively air to headquarters. There were numerous beautiful wreaths sent by the relatives and military friends. Colour-Sergeant T Appleton had entire charge of the arrangeni.nts, and Messrs Walton were the undertikeral The coffin was of Canadian oak, with brass fittings.—The deceased had been Instructor to the Newton Detachment about six years, being transferred from the Wiltshire Regiment in which he bad served about 20 years. He had spent considerable time in India, and had been through the Xulu campaign under Lord Chelmsford.

The harvest Festival in connection with the above place of worship will be held on Sunday. Special anthems will be retriered by the choir, and in the after- noon there will be a bright hour's service, when the Rev W A Eyre will deliver a brief address. For further particulars see advertisement.

St. Luke's Congregational Mission.

The harvest festival in connection with this Mission WAS held on Sanday. There were good congregations at all the services. The place had been very prettily decorated under the superintendence of Mr and Mrs Lowe. In the afternoon service the Rev Sidney Morris preached a v3ry appropriate sermon on the harvest. In the evening the pulpit was occupied by Mr J Miller. A service of song was given on the Monday evening. Mr W Handley occupying the chair. The connective read- ings were read by Mr F Beck. Mr E Penny conducted and the proceedings were a pronounced success.

Bewsey.road Wesleyan Church, Warrington.

Harvest thanksgiving services were held in this church on Sunday. During the latter days of the pre- ceding week, Mrs Hocken, Mrs Wadsworth, Mrs Savage and the Misses E Jones, Collin, Lawton, Healey, Skelland and E Bailey, spent considerable time and trouble in placing to the utmost advantage the gifts of corn, fruit, flowers, and plants, and the result was a credit to their efforts. Never in the history of the church had the interior presented such a pleasing appearance. The preacher in the morning was the Rev W C J Nevlle, who. in his prayer, made a sympathetic reference to the eeath of President fil`Kinley. The choir rendered the anthem " 0 worship the King." Mr B Wadsworth presided at the organ. In the evening the Rev C 13 Hocken conducted the service, end in his dis- course commented upon the spiritual significance of the ,1 harvest festival. The anthems rendered were " While I the earth remaineth " and "0 worship the King." The collections were in excess of those of the previous year.

Wycliffe Congregational Church.

The annual harvest tnankgiving services were held in this place of worship on Sunday. Toe preacher was the Rev John Yonge, who at the morning service referred to the death of the President of the United States, of which a report will be found in another column. In accordance with the usual custom the church was not decorated, but a fine display of flowers and ferns was seen on the communion table. The choir rendered the anthem " Thou visitest the earth," the solo part being efficiently taken by Miss Kershaw. Mr W H Payton Mus Bac presided at the organ, There was an'exceedingly good congregation at:the evening service when the Rev John Yonge spoke from the words " Neither will I offer burnt offerings unto the Lord my God of that which doth cost me nothing " The special 'anthems were " Almighty God" (W H Payton) and " Praise the Lord, 0 Jerusalem " (E V Hall). The sanctus composed by the respected organist of the church was exceedingly well sung, and the music reflec- ted the highest credit upon the composer. Mrs R H Pearson gave a very effective rendering of the difficult solo part in the anthem " Praise the Lord, 0 Jerusa- em." Co I,ctions were taken on behalf of the Church Contingent Fu )d,

St. Paul's, Runcorn.

The annual festival in connection with the above church was held on Sunday and Monday. For the week previous to the festival the ladies and gentlemen associated with the church had been strenuously at work on the fbrai decorations in a praiseworthy effort to maintain the church's hitherto high tradition. The result was all that could be expected. The interior of the sacred edifice presented an artistic as well as a picturesque ap- pearance and the workers deserve something more than a passing word of prai:e for their skilful treatment. St. Paul's doe: not readily lend itself to artistic decoration, being rather too spacious and roomy for compact dis- play but contrivance and perseverance overcame all obstacles. The pillars had been prettily draped with strings of green foliage out of which peeped delicate flowers while between them hung dainty baskets corn- posed of ferns, creepers and exquisite plants. The winnows downstairs, too, had. been very neatly dressed the floral designs being considerably enhanced with the different species of fruit with which they were dressed. Round the walls were hung many suitable shields indica- tive of " Harvest Home.." The prettiest were, " The Kirg reigeeth" surmounting a beautifully worked crown of dowers and a sickle attached to a handful of wheat. The communion table and rail looked handsome In the extreme. Round the rail was hung a deep fringe of oats which was in turn crested with asparagus fern and asters, which were prettily looped with small red crabs hanging from spikes of red gladiolas. The communion table's chief ornament was a large basket of flowers from the handle of which hung a bunch of luseious grapes and which was surrounded with lilies and other foliage. On the right hand side of the table Was an exquisite vase studded wi'h violet asters and relieved with white stocks fringed with white and pink Mice. On the other side stood a large wheatsheaf while round the pulpit were trouped agricultural imp ements. The pulpit was the most striking centre-piece, round the bottom being hung cactus dahlias from which were suspended asparagus fern. The middle panel contained the text of the fe,tival, "For the harvest is full," which encir- cled a crown of white lilies worked on green. On the other sides, fringed with the olive leaf were panels of white asters worked on red baize, the whole of the de- sign being choicely touched with delicate ferns and fruit. Fronting the choir stalls was a beautiful belting of asparagas fern festooned with Small red crabs which were looped on spikes of flowers from which fruit was suspended. The organ seat was faced with a beautiful harp made of oats and wheat, the strings being com- posed of small red crab apples. Branching the instru- ment was a wealth of green foliage intermingled with bulrushes. There were good congregatione at all the services. In the morning the Bev S B Foster delivered a-very instructive sermon on the tem" "That both grow Ja together until e harvest." The minister ormetualized the parable of the tares and the wheat, and gave a very pleasing analogy between the sowing and reaping of the harvest and et-Afloat judgment of mankind.—At the evening service the Rev A WEN occupied the pulpit and I delivered an eloquent sermon on the words ‘' If I had not come and spoken unto them they had not load sin but now they have no cloke for their sin!, The great lesson from the text, the minister said, was that oppor- tunity WAS the measure of responsibility. Circumstances and motives were the two great factors in the shaping of life.—On Monday the services were concluded, the Rev G B Saul (Widnes) oceupying the pulpit. He de- livered a very instructive discourse from Isaiah x win. 4-5 The minister's subj:ct was " Providence " and he showed how the Christian ought to trust on in the higher power in spite of the fact that evil seemed, at times, to gain tbe ascendancy. God's Providence worked everything out right. God never slept though be sometimes rested.—The anthems were splendidly rendered be the choir and comprised, " Oh ! give thanks." "Oh Worship the King,"" They that dwell in the land "and " Praise the Lord." The soloists Messrs J Leathwood, E Kniveton, T Johnson and Miss M A Jones acquitted themselves with distinction and added considerably to the success of the proceedings. The collections were satisfactory.

HESHIRE COUNTY COUNCIL. BY ELECTION, 1901. APPLBTON RLECTOBAL DIVISION. LADIES AND GENTLEMAN', A vacancy has arisen in the representatiola of this Division of the Cheshire County Council. I have been favoured with a numerously signed request of Electors in all parts of the Constituency to become a 'Candidate kir the seat and have much pleasure In placing my services at your disposal. Should you oo me the honour of electing me as your representative. I assure you that It will always be my earnest endeavour to the best of my ability to promote and further the interests and welfare of the Division. I am, Ladies and Gentlemen, Yours faithfully, ROGER CriARLTON PAUL. Grappenhall Heyes, 6th September, 93.

A IDING AND ABETTING POACHERS -Ann Stubbs, an old woman, Clifton Cottages, was summoned for unlawfully aiding some one wha had been in search of game on the 11th september.-P,C. Rowlands stated that on Wednesday when In Rocksavage-road Clifton, he noticed something uuder defandant's cape. He seirched ter and found a young rabbit. WATER POLO. League—First Division. LIVERPOOL CITY , V WARRINGTON- These teams met yesterday week at Burroughs-gardens Baths, Liverpool. Warrington were short of W Ashton, otherwise both teams were at their full strength. At the start the visitors defending the shallow end, worked up the bath, and alter some give and take play &scow secured, and easily defeated Turner, the City's goal- keeper. Turning round at half time the City set to work, and scored two goals in grand style. The home team after this success had all the play, but could not add to their score, a good game ending in a victory by two g oals to one. fit C C Snowden, hen secretary Liverpool anti District, acted as referee, and Mr J F Finegan as timekeeper. • A severe shock of earthquake was felt at twenty-five minutes past one o'clock on Wednesday morning at Inverness. The stuck lasted about three seconds. Much alarm was caused, and s.mie chimney-tops fell into the streets, but no serious damage was done. The earthquake was felt also at different places from Aber- deen to Dornock. At the Pencoed Collieries, Carmarthen, on Wednesday after the firing of a shot, there was a tremendous ex- plosion, and seven miners were , injured. 'I bele clothes were completely burned away, and their flesh came off in large pieces. Damage estimated at between £6,000 and £7,000 was caused by fire at Messrs W Lucas and Co.'s hat works at Chesham, Bury, on Wednesday morning.

THE PROPOSED SMALL-PDX HOSPITAL. --- Decision by the Local Government Board. The Town Clerk has received a communication from the Local tiavernment Board authorising the expenditure of .£10,129 upon the provision of a small-pox hospital at Great Sankey. The application of the Corporation was for the expenditure of a sum of £9.979, but several alter- ',ions suggested by the Local Government Beard will increase the estimated expenditure to £10,129, which sum has been sanctioned by the Board. The Corporation have also succeeded in their applica- tion to the Local Government Board for sanction to borrow £8,702 for extensiocs of the infectious diseases hospital in Aikin-street. This was the sum applied for. An inquiry will be conducted at the Town Hall on Friday m6rning next, commencing at 10-30 by Mr M K North, AM IC H. an inspector of the Local Government Board into the application of the Corporation for sanction to borrow- £15,000 for purposes of electric lighing.
